, else
c=a\cdot b+\delta
, where
\delta\neq 0
.
Moreover, the adversary does not learn anything additional about
a
and
b
in the protocol.
The protocol also takes input an
iteration number

\mathsf{iter}
and all the sets computed in the protocol are tagged with
\mathsf{iter}
.
Looking ahead, our
robust
triple-generation protocol will be executed
iteratively
, with each iteration invoking instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
.

A detailed overview of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
has been already presented in Section
1.2.6
. The idea is to let each summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
be linearly secret-shared
by exactly one
summand-sharing
party. A secret-sharing of
a\cdot b
then follows from
the secret-sharing of each summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
owing to the linearity property of the secret-sharing.
To deal with the network agnostic condition, the summand-sharing parties are selected in two phases:
first
, we select them
dynamically
,
without
pre-assigning any summand to any
designated party. Once there exists a subset of parties from
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
who have served the role of summand-sharing parties, we go to the
second
phase,
where each remaining summand is designated to the left-over parties through some publicly known assignment. Strict timeouts are maintained
to ensure that we don’t stuck forever during the second phase.
Finally, if there are still any remaining summands which are not yet secret-shared, they are publicly reconstructed and the default sharing is taken on their behalf.
Throughout, the parties in
\mathcal{GD}
are not let to secret-share any summand, since they are
already known
to be
corrupt
and at the same time, it is ensured that the shares
of the honest parties are
never
publicly reconstructed.

\justify

–

Initialization
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}
do the following.

\bullet

Initialize the
summand-index-set
of indices of
all
summands:

\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\{(p,q)\}_{p,q=1,\ldots,|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|}.

\bullet

Initialize the
summand-index-set
corresponding to each
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
:

\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\{(p,q)\}_{P_{j}\in S_{p}\cap S_{q}}.

\bullet

Initialize the
summand-index-set
corresponding to each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
:

\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{q})}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\displaystyle\cup_{P_{j}\in S_{q}}\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}.

\bullet

Initialize the set of summands-sharing parties:

\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\emptyset.

\bullet

Initialize the hop number:

\mathsf{hop}=1.

\justify

Phase I: Sharing Summands Through Dynamic Assignment

–

While there exists
no

S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, where
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{q})}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\emptyset
, the parties do the following:

\bullet

Sharing Sum of Eligible Summands
: Every
P_{i}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
invokes an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},i)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}

with input
c^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, where

\displaystyle c^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}}[a]_{p}[b]_{q}.

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
, the parties in
\mathcal{P}
participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
, if invoked by
P_{j}
.

\bullet

Selecting Summand-Sharing Party for the Hop Through ACS
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}

wait for time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
and then do the following.

–

For
j=1,\ldots,n
, participate in an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}

corresponding to
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
with input
1
if
all
the following hold:

–

P_{j}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
;

–

An output
[c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
is computed during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
.

–

Upon computing an output
1
during the instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
corresponding to some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, participate with input
0

in the instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},k)}
corresponding to parties
P_{k}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
, for which no input has been provided yet.

–

Upon computing outputs during the instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},i)}
corresponding to each
P_{i}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
,
let
P_{j}
be the least-indexed party, such that the output
1
is computed during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
. Then update the following.

–

\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\{P_{j}\}
.

–

\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}\setminus\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

\forall P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{\mathcal{GD}\cup\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\}
:

\mathsf{SIS}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathsf{SIS}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}\setminus\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

For each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
,
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{q})}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{q})}_{\mathsf{iter}}\setminus\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

Set
\mathsf{hop}=\mathsf{hop}+1
.

\justify

Phase II: Sharing Remaining Summands Through Static Assignment
\justify

\bullet

Re-assigning the Summand-Index-Set of Each Party
: Corresponding to each
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the parties in
\mathcal{P}

set
\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
as

\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\displaystyle\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cap\{(p,q)\}_{P_{j}=\min(S_{p}\cap S_{q})},

where
\min(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
denotes the minimum indexed party in
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
.

\bullet

Sharing Sum of Assigned Summands
: Every party
P_{i}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})

invokes an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phII},i)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
with input
c^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, where

\displaystyle c^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}}[a]_{p}[b]_{q}.

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
,
the parties in
\mathcal{P}
participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
, if invoked by
P_{j}
.

\bullet

Agreeing on the Summand-Sharing parties of the Second Phase
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}

wait for
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
time after the beginning of the second phase
.
Then for each
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, participate in an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
with input
1
, if
all
the following hold, otherwise
participate with input
0
.

–

P_{j}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
;

–

An output
[c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
is computed during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
.

\bullet

Updating the Sets for the Second Phase
: Corresponding to each
P_{j}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
, such that
1
is computed as the output during

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
, update

–

\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}\setminus\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
;

–

\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\{P_{j}\}
.
\justify

Phase III: Reconstructing the Remaining Summands
\justify

\bullet

Reconstructing the Remaining Summands and Taking the Default Sharing
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}
do the following.

–

Corresponding to each
[a]_{p}
such that
(p,\star)\in\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, participate in the instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}([a],S_{p},\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}
to publicly reconstruct
[a]_{p}

–

Corresponding to each
[b]_{q}
such that
(\star,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, participate in the instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}([b],S_{q},\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}
to publicly reconstruct
[b]_{q}
.

–

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, take the
default linear secret-sharing
of
the public input
c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
with IC-signatures and core-sets
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
,
where
16
16

16

The default linear secret-sharing of
c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
is computed in a similar way as done in the protocol

\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
for
\mathbf{s}
(see Fig
9
).

\displaystyle c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}}[a]_{p}[b]_{q}.

\bullet

Output Computation
: The parties output

({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[c^{(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\ldots,[c^{(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\allowbreak[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])
, where
c_{\mathsf{iter}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}c^{(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+\ldots+c^{(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

Figure 11
:
Network-agnostic non-robust multiplication protocol

The properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
are claimed in the following lemmas, which are proved in Appendix
0.F
.

Lemma 7

During any instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},[a],[b],{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
,
if
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
then
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{GD}
, irrespective of the network type.

Lemma 8

Suppose that no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
. If the honest parties start participating during hop number
\mathsf{hop}
of Phase I of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
with iteration number
\mathsf{iter}
,
then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
the hop takes
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
time to complete in a synchronous network, or almost-surely completes eventually in an asynchronous network.

Lemma 9

If no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
, then in protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
all honest parties compute some output by the time
T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}=(2n+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{BA}}+(n+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network, or almost-surely,
eventually in an asynchronous network.

Lemma 10

If no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
, then the view of the adversary remains independent of
a
and
b
throughout the protocol, irrespective of the network type.

Lemma 11

If no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
and if all parties in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
behave honestly, then in protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, the honest parties output
a linear secret-sharing of
a\cdot b
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, irrespective of the network type.

Lemma 12

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{5}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{6}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

As a corollary of Lemma
12
, we can derive the following corollary, which determines the
maximum
number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
which are invoked during an instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
. Looking ahead, this will be useful to later calculate the maximum number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
which need to be invoked as part of our final multiplication protocol.
This will be further useful to determine the number of linearly secret-shared values with IC-signatures and core-sets
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
, which need to be generated through the
protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
beforehand.

Corollary 1

During any instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, there can be at most
n^{2}+n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
for
L
Pairs of Inputs.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
can be easily generalized, if there are
L
pairs of inputs
\{(a^{\ell},b^{\ell})\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L}
, all of which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. However, with a slight modification during Phase I and Phase II, we can ensure that the number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
remain
only

\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
, which is
independent
of
L
. Consider Phase I. During hop number
\mathsf{hop}
, every party
P_{i}\not\in(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
invokes
L
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
to linearly secret-share

L
candidate summand-sums. Now while selecting the summand-sharing party through ACS for this hop, the parties vote for a candidate
P_{j}\not\in(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
, provided an output is computed in

all
the
L
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
. Consequently, the number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
during Phase I will be
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.
Similarly during Phase II, each party
outside

(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
invokes
L
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
to linearly secret-share
L
candidate re-assigned summand-sums. And then the parties vote for a
candidate
P_{j}
as a summand-sharing party, if an output is computed in
all
the
L
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
.
Finally, during Phase III, the default linear secret-sharing with IC-signatures is taken for the sum of all the summands, which are not yet secret-shared by any party, by making public all these summands.
The resultant protocol incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|^{3}\cdot n^{4}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{5}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{6}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
. We also note that there will be at most
n^{2}\cdot L+n\cdot L
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked in the generalized protocol.
To avoid repetition, we do not present the steps of the generalized protocol here.

8.3
Network Agnostic Random Triple Generation with Cheater Identification

The network-agnostic protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
(Fig
12
) takes an iteration number
\mathsf{iter}
and a publicly known subset of parties
\mathcal{GD}
, who
are guaranteed to be
corrupt
. If
all
the parties in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}

behave
honestly
, then the protocol outputs a random linearly secret-shared multiplication-triple with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core sets.
Otherwise, with a high probability, the honest parties identify a
new corrupt
party, which is added to
\mathcal{GD}
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
is based on
[
40
]
and
consists of two stages: during the
first
stage, the parties jointly generate a pair of random values, which are
linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core sets.
During the second stage, the parties run an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
to compute the product of the pair of secret-shared random values from the first stage.
To check whether any cheating has occurred during the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, the parties then run a probabilistic test, namely
the “sacrificing trick”
[
27
]
,
for which the parties need
additional
secret-shared random values, which
are generated during the first stage itself.

\justify

–

Generating Linear Secret Sharing of Random Values with IC-signatures
: Each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
does the following.

–

Invoke instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
with randomly chosen inputs
a^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}},r^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}\in\mathbb{F}
.

–

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, participate in the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
(if any)
and
wait for time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
.
Initialize a set
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\emptyset

after local time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
and include
P_{j}
in

\mathcal{C}_{i}
, if any output is computed in
all
the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
.

–

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
,
participate in an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
with input
1
, if
P_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

–

Once
1
has been computed as the output from instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
corresponding to a set of parties
in
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, participate
with input
0
in all the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
, such that

P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

–

Once a binary output is computed in all the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
corresponding to the parties in
\mathcal{P}
,
compute
\mathcal{CS}
, which is the set of parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, such that

1
is computed as the output in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
.

Once
\mathcal{CS}
is computed, the parties in
\mathcal{P}
locally compute
[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[r_{\mathsf{iter}}]
from

\{[a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}},\{[b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}},\{[b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}
and
\{[r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}
respectively as follows:

[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]=\displaystyle\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}[a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\;[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]=\displaystyle\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}[b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\;[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]=\displaystyle\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}[b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\;[r_{\mathsf{iter}}]=\displaystyle\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}[r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}].

–

Computing Secret-Shared Products
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}
do the following.

–

Participate in instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\allowbreak\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})

and
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},[a],[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}],{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}

to compute the outputs
({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[c^{(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\ldots,[c^{(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])
and

({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[c^{\prime(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\ldots,[c^{\prime(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}])
respectively.
Let
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c}
and
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}
be the summand-sharing parties for the two instances respectively.
Moreover, for each
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c}
, let
\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}

be the set of ordered pairs of indices corresponding to the summands whose sum has been shared by
P_{j}

during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
.
And similarly, for each
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}
, let
\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}

be the set of ordered pairs of indices corresponding to the summands whose sum has been shared by
P_{j}

during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},[a],[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}],{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
.

–

Error Detection in the Instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}
do the following.

–

Upon computing outputs from the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, participate in an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}([r_{\mathsf{iter}}],\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
to publicly reconstruct
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

Locally compute
[e_{\mathsf{iter}}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}r_{\mathsf{iter}}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]+[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
from
[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
. Participate in an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}([e_{\mathsf{iter}}],\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}

to publicly reconstruct
e_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

Locally compute
[d_{\mathsf{iter}}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}e_{\mathsf{iter}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]-r_{\mathsf{iter}}[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]-[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
from
[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
.
Participate in an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}([d_{\mathsf{iter}}],\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
to publicly reconstruct
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

Output Computation in Case of Success:

If
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
, then set the boolean variable
\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
and output
{({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])}
.

–

Cheater Identification in Case of Failure:
If
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
, then set the boolean variable

\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}=1
and proceed as follows.

–

For each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, participate in instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}([a_{\mathsf{iter}}],S_{q},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}),\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}([b_{\mathsf{iter}}],S_{q},\mathcal{P})
and

\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}([b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}],S_{q},\mathcal{P})

of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}
to publicly reconstruct the shares
\{[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q},[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q},[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}\}_{S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}}
.
In addition, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, participate in instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}(c^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}},\mathcal{P})
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}(c^{\prime(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}},\mathcal{P})
to publicly reconstruct
c^{(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c^{\prime(i)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

\bullet

Set
\displaystyle\mathcal{GD}=\mathcal{GD}\cup\{P_{j}\}
, if
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c}\cup\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}

and the following holds for
P_{j}
:

\displaystyle r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}+\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}.

Figure 12
:
Network-agnostic protocol for generating secret-shared random multiplication-triple with cheater
identification.

The properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}

.

Lemma 13

In protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
, the following hold.

–

Synchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, honest parties will have linearly secret-shared
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}

a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

Asynchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely, honest parties will eventually have linearly secret-shared
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}

a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

Lemma 18

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{5}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{6}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
for
L
Triples.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
can be easily generalized to generate
L
triples with cheater identification, such that the number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
is
independent
of
L
.
To begin with, every party
P_{i}
now picks
3L+1
random values
(r_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(i)},\{a_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell,i)},b_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell,i)},{b^{\prime}}_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell,i)}\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L})

and invokes
3L+1
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
to generate linear secret-sharing of these values with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Next, to determine
\mathcal{CS}
, the parties invoke
only

n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
, where the
j^{th}
instance is used
to decide whether
P_{j}
should be included in
\mathcal{CS}
, the criteria being whether some output is computed in
all
the
3L+1
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
.
Once
\mathcal{CS}
is decided, the parties locally compute
\{[a_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell)}]\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L}
,
\{[b_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell)}]\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L}
,
\{[{b^{\prime}}_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell)}]\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L}

and
[r_{\mathsf{iter}}]
from
\{[a_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell,j)}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS},\ell=1,\ldots,L}
,
\{[b_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell,j)}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS},\ell=1,\ldots,L}
,

\{[{b^{\prime}}_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell,j)}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS},\ell=1,\ldots,L}
and
\{[r_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(j)}]\}_{P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}}
respectively.
The parties then invoke two instances of (generalized)
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
protocol with
L
pairs of secret-shared inputs to compute

\{[c_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell)}]\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L}
and
\{[{c^{\prime}}_{\mathsf{iter}}^{(\ell)}]\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,L}
respectively. Note that this requires
only

\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
, apart from
n^{2}\cdot L+n\cdot L

instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
. The rest of the protocol steps are then generalized to deal with
L
inputs.
The resultant protocol incurs a communication of

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{5}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{6}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
. We also note that there will be at most
n^{2}\cdot L+4n\cdot L+n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked overall in the generalized protocol.
To avoid repetition, we do not present the steps of the generalized protocol here.

8.4
The Multiplication-Triple Generation Protocol

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
for generating a single secret-shared multiplication-triple is presented in Fig
13
. The idea of the protocol is very simple and based on
[
40
]
.
The parties iteratively run instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
, till they hit upon an instance when
no
cheating is detected.
Corresponding to each “failed” instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
, the parties keep updating the set
\mathcal{GD}
. Since after each failed instance the set
\mathcal{GD}
is updated with
one
new
corrupt party, there will be at most
(t+1)
iterations, where
t
is the cardinality of the largest-sized subset in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.

\justify

–

Initialization
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}
initialize
\mathcal{GD}=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{iter}=1
.

–

Triple Generation with Cheater Identification
: The parties in
\mathcal{P}
participate in an instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
and wait for its completion.
Upon computing output from the instance, the parties proceed as follows.

\bullet

Positive Output
: If the Boolean variable
\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}
is set to
0
during the instance of

\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
, then
output
{({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])}
, computed during the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
.

\bullet

Negative Output
: Else set
\mathsf{iter}=\mathsf{iter}+1
and go to the step labelled
Triple Generation with Cheater Identification
.

Figure 13
:
Network-agnostic protocol to generate a linear
secret sharing with IC-signature of a single random multiplication-triple.

The properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
are claimed in the following lemmas, which are proved in Appendix
0.F
.

Lemma 19

Let
t
be the size of the largest set in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the honest parties compute an output during

\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, by the time
T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}=(t+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
in a synchronous network, or almost-surely, eventually in an asynchronous network, where

T_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}=T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+4T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
.

Lemma 20

If the honest parties output
{({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])}
during the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
,
then
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c_{\mathsf{iter}}
are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Moreover,
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}b_{\mathsf{iter}}
holds, except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.
Furthermore, the view of the adversary remains independent of
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

Lemma 21

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{6}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{7}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
for Generating
L
Multiplication-Triples.

To generate
L
multiplication-triples, the parties now need to invoke an instance of the generalized (modified)
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
protocol in each iteration,
which generates
L
triples with cheater identification.
The rest of the protocol steps remain the same. To avoid repetition, we do not present the formal details here.
The protocol incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{6}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{7}\cdot L\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

On the Maximum Number of Calls of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
.

As discussed in the previous section, each instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
for
L
triples requires at most

n^{2}\cdot L+4n\cdot L+n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
. Now as there can be up to
t+1\approx n
such instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, it follows that
at most
n^{3}\cdot L+4n^{2}\cdot L+n^{2}
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
are invoked in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
for generating
L
multiplication-triples.

9
Network Agnostic Circuit-EvaluationProtocol

The network-agnostic circuit-evaluation protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
is presented in Fig
14
.
The idea behind the protocol is to perform shared circuit-evaluation, where each value remains linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures and common core-sets
Once the function-output is secret-shared, it is publicly reconstructed. To achieve this goal, the parties first secret-share their respective inputs for the function
f
through instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
.
The parties then agree on a common subset of parties
\mathcal{CS}
, where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that the inputs of the parties in
\mathcal{CS}
are linearly secret-shared
with IC-signatures. If the network is
synchronous
then it will be ensured that
all honest
parties are present in
\mathcal{CS}

and hence the inputs of
all honest
parties are considered for the circuit-evaluation.
The linearity of secret-sharing ensures that the linear gates in
\mathsf{ckt}
are evaluated non-interactively, while Beaver’s trick is deployed for evaluating multiplication gates in
\mathsf{ckt}
.
For the latter, the parties need to have
c_{M}
number of random multiplication-triples apriori, which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures.
This is achieved by apriori calling the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
with
L=c_{M}
, which in turn will require
at most

n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}
number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
.
As the
total
number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
across
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
and the input-phase is at most
n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
,
the parties first invoke an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}

by setting
L=n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
, to generate
these many linearly secret-shared random values with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
This will ensure that all the values during the circuit-evaluation are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

Notice that if the network is
asynchronous
then different parties may be in the different phases of the protocol. And consequently, a party upon reconstructing the function output
cannot

afford to immediately terminate, as its presence may be required in the other parts of the protocol. Hence there is also a
termination phase
, which is executed concurrently, where the parties check if
it is “safe” to terminate the protocol.

\justify

Pre-Processing Phase

–

Generating Linearly Secret-Shared Random Values with IC-signatures
: The parties invoke an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},L)

where
L=n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n

and compute output

({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\{[r^{(\mathfrak{l})}]\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,L})

–

Generating Linearly Secret-Shared Random Multiplication-Triples with IC-signatures
:
Upon computing an output in the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
,
the parties invoke an instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|})
with
L=c_{M}

and compute output
{({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\{[a^{(\ell)}],[b^{(\ell)}],[c^{(\ell)}]\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,c_{M}})}
.

During the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, the secret-shared values
\{[r^{(\mathfrak{l})}]\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}}

are used as the corresponding pads in the underlying instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, invoked as part of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
.

Input Phase

Upon computing an output during the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
does the following.

–

On having the input
x^{(i)}
, invoke an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
with input

x^{(i)}
.
17
17

17

The secret-shared random value

r^{(n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+j)}
serves as the pad for the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
in this phase.

–

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, participate in the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}

and
wait for local time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
after starting the input phase
. Then
initialize a set
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\emptyset
and include
P_{j}
in

\mathcal{C}_{i}
, if any output is computed in the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
.

–

Corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
,
participate in an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
with input
1
, if
P_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

–

Once
1
has been computed as the output from instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
corresponding to a set of parties
in
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, participate
with input
0
in all the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
, such that

P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

–

Once a binary output is computed in all the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
corresponding to the parties in
\mathcal{P}
,
compute
\mathcal{CS}
, which is the set of parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, such that

1
is computed as the output in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
.

Once
\mathcal{CS}
is computed, corresponding to every
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{CS}
, the parties set
x^{(j)}=0
and
take the
default

linear secret-sharing of
0
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

Circuit-Evaluation Phase

–

Evaluate each gate
g
in the circuit
according to the topological ordering as follows, depending upon the type.

–

Addition Gate
: If
g
is an addition gate with inputs
x,y
and output
z
, then the parties in
\mathcal{P}
locally compute
[z]=[x+y]
from
[x]
and
[y]
.

–

Multiplication Gate
: If
g
is the
\ell^{th}
multiplication gate with inputs
x,y
and output
z
, where
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, then
the parties in
\mathcal{P}
do the following:

–

Locally compute
[d^{(\ell)}]=[x-a^{(\ell)}]
from
[x]
and
[a^{(\ell)}]
and

[e^{(\ell)}]=[y-b^{(\ell)}]
from
[y]
and
[b^{(\ell)}]
.

–

Participate in instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}([d^{(\ell)}],\mathcal{P})
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}([e^{(\ell)}],\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}

to publicly reconstruct

d^{(\ell)}
and
e^{(\ell)}
, where
d^{(\ell)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}x-a^{(\ell)}
and
e^{(\ell)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}y-b^{(\ell)}
.

–

Upon reconstructing
d^{(\ell)}
and
e^{(\ell)}
,
take the default linear secret-sharing of
d^{(\ell)}\cdot e^{(\ell)}
with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets
. Then
locally compute

[z]=[d^{(\ell)}\cdot e^{(\ell)}]+d^{(\ell)}\cdot[b^{(\ell)}]+e^{(\ell)}\cdot[a^{(\ell)}]+[c^{(\ell)}]
from

[d^{(\ell)}\cdot e^{(\ell)}],[a^{(\ell)}],[b^{(\ell)}]
and
[c^{(\ell)}]
.

–

Output Gate
: If
g
is the output gate with output
y
, then participate in an instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}([y],\mathcal{P})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
to publicly
reconstruct
y
.

Termination Phase

Every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
concurrently executes the following steps during the protocol:

–

Upon computing the circuit-output
y
, send the message
(\mathsf{ready},P_{i},y)
to every party in
\mathcal{P}
.

–

Upon receiving the message
(\mathsf{ready},P_{j},y)
from a set of parties
{\mathcal{}A}
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}({\mathcal{}A},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
send
(\mathsf{ready},P_{i},y)
to every party in
\mathcal{P}
, provided no
(\mathsf{ready},P_{i},\star)
message has been sent yet.

–

Upon receiving the message
(\mathsf{ready},P_{j},y)
from a set of parties
\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{W}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, output
y
and terminate.

:
Network agnostic secure circuit-evaluation protocol.

The properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
stated in Theorem
9.1
, are proved in Appendix
0.G
.

Theorem 9.1

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be monotone adversary structures where
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, the set

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfy the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
, the set
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(3)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a}
together satisfy the

\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition.
Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finite field such that
|\mathbb{F}|\geq n^{5}\cdot 2^{\mathsf{ssec}}
where

\mathsf{ssec}
is the statistical security parameter. Moreover, let
y=f(x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(n)})
be a publicly known function over
\mathbb{F}
represented by an arithmetic circuit
\mathsf{ckt}
over
\mathbb{F}
,
where each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
has the input
x^{(i)}
. Furthermore, let
c_{M}
and
D_{M}
be the number of multiplication gates and the multiplicative depth of
\mathsf{ckt}
respectively.
Then given an unconditionally-secure PKI,
protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
achieves the following, where every honest
P_{j}
participates with the input
x^{(j)}
and where
t
denotes the cardinality of the maximum sized subset in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.

–

Synchronous Network
: Except with probability
2^{-\mathsf{ssec}}
, all honest parties output
y=f(x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(n)})
at the time

[(3n+5)t^{2}+(74n+140)t+69n+D_{M}+438]\cdot\Delta
, where
x^{(j)}=0
, for every
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{CS}
and where every honest party is present in
\mathcal{CS}
, such that

\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.

–

Asynchronous Network
: Except with probability
2^{-\mathsf{ssec}}
, almost-surely, all honest parties eventually output
y=f(x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(n)})

where
x^{(j)}=0
, for every
P_{j}\not\in CoreSet
and where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.

The protocol incurs a communication of

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|^{2}\cdot n^{12}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|})

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.
Moreover, irrespective of the network type, the view of the adversary remains independent of the inputs of the honest parties in
\mathcal{CS}
.

10
Impossibility Result

Here we show the necessity of the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition for network agnostic MPC. In fact we show that the condition is even necessary for network agnostic BA. For this, we generalize the
impossibility proof of
[
15
]
which shows the impossibility of network agnostic BA against threshold adversaries if
2t_{s}+t_{a}\geq n
.

Theorem 10.1

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the

\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
and
\mathbb{Q}^{(3)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
conditions respectively, where
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
18
18

18

The necessity of the

\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
and
\mathbb{Q}^{(3)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
conditions follow from the existing results on the impossibility of unconditionally-secure SBA and ABA respectively, without
these conditions. The condition
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
is also necessary since any potential corrupt subset which is tolerable in an
asynchronous
network should also be tolerable if
the network is
synchronous
.

Moreover, let the parties have access to the setup of an unconditional PKI.
Furthermore,
let
\Pi
be an
n
-party protocol, which is a
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-secure SBA protocol in the synchronous network and which is a
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-secure ABA protocol in the asynchronous network (as per Definition
1
).
Then
\Pi
exists only if
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition.

Proof

The proof is by contradiction. Let
\Pi
exist, even if
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}

do not
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition.
Then, there exist sets, say
Z_{0},Z_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
Z_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
such that
Z_{0}\cup Z_{1}\cup Z_{2}\supseteq\mathcal{P}
holds.
For simplicity and without loss of generality, assume that
Z_{0},Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are
disjoint
.
Now consider the following executions of
\Pi
.
In
all
these executions, parties in
Z_{0}
participate with input
0
, and parties in
Z_{1}
participate with input
1
.

–

Execution
E_{1}
: In this execution, the network is
synchronous
. All the parties in
Z_{0}
are corrupted by the adversary and simply
abort
,
and all parties in
Z_{2}
participate with input
1
. Since
all
the
honest
parties (namely the parties in
Z_{1}\cup Z_{2}
) have input
1
, from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi
in the

synchronous
network, the parties in
Z_{1}\cup\mathcal{Z}_{2}
should output
1
after some fixed time, say
T_{1}
.

–

Execution
E_{2}
: In this execution, the network is
synchronous
. All the parties in
Z_{1}
are corrupted by the adversary and simply
abort
,
and all parties in
Z_{2}
participate with input
0
. Since
all
the
honest
parties (namely the parties in
Z_{0}\cup Z_{2}
) have input
0
, from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi
in the

synchronous
network, the parties in
Z_{0}\cup\mathcal{Z}_{2}
should output
0
after some fixed time, say
T_{2}
.

–

Execution
E_{3}
: In this execution, the network is
asynchronous
, the adversary corrupts all the
parties in
Z_{2}
and behave as follows:
the communication between the parties in
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
is delayed by at least time
\mbox{max}(T_{1},T_{2})
.
The adversary communicates with parties in
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
, such that the views of the parties in
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
are identical to
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
respectively.
For this, the adversary runs
\Pi
with input
0
when interacting with the parties in
Z_{0}
and runs

\Pi
with input
1
when interacting with the parties in
Z_{1}
.
Hence, the parties in
Z_{0}
output
0
, while the parties in
Z_{1}
output
1
, which violates the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency
of
\Pi
in the
asynchronous
network. This is a contradiction and hence,
\Pi

does not
exist.

11
Acknowledgements

We would like to thank Anirudh Chandramouli for several helpful discussions during the early stages of this research.

Appendix 0.A
Properties of the Network Agnostic BA Protocol

In this section, we prove the properties of the network agnostic BA protocol (see Fig
1
for the protocol).
We first formally present the sub-protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
and prove its properties.

0.A.1
Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
: Synchronous BA with Asynchronous Guaranteed Liveness

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
is presented in Fig
15
, where for simplicity we assume that the input of each party is a bit.
The protocol is very simple.
Each party
P_{i}
uses a Dolev-Strong (DS) style protocol
[
30
]

to broadcast its input
b_{i}
. The protocol runs for
t+1
“rounds”, where
t
is the size of the largest set in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Each party
P_{i}
accumulates values on the behalf of every party
P_{j}
in a set
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
. A bit
b
is added to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
during round
r
only if
P_{i}
receives signatures on
b
from
r
distinct parties

including

P_{j}
. Party
P_{i}
computes the final output by taking the “majority” among the accumulated values. This is done by computing a final set
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
of values based on each
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
set.
Since the DS protocol is designed for the
synchronous
network, for convenience,
we present the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
in a round-based fashion, where the parties set the duration of each round to
\Delta
and
will know
the
beginning and end of each round.

\justify

–

Initialization
: Initialize the following sets.

–

For
j=1,\ldots,n
: the set of values accumulated on the behalf of
P_{j}
,
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}=\emptyset
.

–

t
: size of the largest set in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.

–

The final set of values to be considered while computing the output
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}=\emptyset
.

–

Round 0
: On having input bit
b_{i}
, sign
b_{i}
to obtain the signature
\sigma_{ii}
. Set
\mathsf{SET}_{i}=\{\sigma_{ii}\}
and send
(b_{i},i,\mathsf{SET}_{i})
to every party
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
.

–

Round
r
=
0
to
t+1
: On receiving
(b,j,\mathsf{SET}_{j})
in round
r
, check if
all
the following hold.

–

\mathsf{SET}_{j}
contains valid signatures on
b
from
r

distinct
parties,
including

P_{j}
;

–

b\notin\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
.

If all the above hold, then do the following.

–

Add
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
.

–

If
r\neq(t+1)
, then compute a signature
\sigma_{ij}
on
b
and send
(b,j,\mathsf{SET}_{j}\cup\{\sigma_{ij}\})
to every party
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
.

–

Output Computation
: At time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
, do the following.

–

If
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
contains exactly one value
b
, then add
(j,b)
to
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
. Else, add
(j,\bot)
to
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
.

–

If there exists a set
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and a value
b\neq\bot
such that, for every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
,
(j,b)
belongs to
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
, then output
b
. Else, output
\bot
.

Figure 15
:
Synchronous BA with asynchronous guaranteed liveness. The above code is executed by every party
P_{i}
with input
b_{i}
.

We next prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
, which are a straightforward generalization of the properties of the DS protocol.

Lemma 22

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
achieves
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Consistency in a synchronous network.

Proof

We claim that each
honest
party
P_{i}
computes the same set
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
corresponding to
every

party
P_{j}
, by time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
. Assuming the claim is true,
the proof then follows from the fact that
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
is computed
deterministically
at the time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
,
based on the sets
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
.
To prove the claim, consider an
arbitrary
honest party
P_{i}
and an
arbitrary

P_{j}
.
If
P_{i}
includes
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
, then we show that by the time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
, the value
b
will be present in the set
\mathsf{ACC}_{kj}
of
every
honest party
P_{k}
.
For this, we consider the following two cases.

–

Case 1 -
P_{i}
added
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
during round
r
where
r\leq t
:
In this case,
P_{i}
must have received
(b,j,\mathsf{SET}_{j})
in round
r
, where
\mathsf{SET}_{j}
contained valid signatures on
b
from
r-1
distinct parties
apart
from
P_{j}
. Party

P_{i}
then computes
\sigma_{ij}
, adds this to
\mathsf{SET}_{j}
, and sends
(b,j,\mathsf{SET}_{j})
to every party. When
P_{k}
receives this during round
r+1
, it will find that
\mathsf{SET}_{j}
contains
r
valid signatures on
b
apart from party
P_{j}
’s, including
\sigma_{ij}
. Hence,
P_{k}
will add
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{kj}
. Since
r+1\leq t+1
, this happens by time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
.

–

Case 2 -
P_{i}
added
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
during round
r=t+1
:
In this case
P_{i}
must have received
(b,j,\mathsf{SET}_{j})
in round
t+1
, where
\mathsf{SET}_{j}
contained valid signatures on
b
from
t
distinct parties
apart
from
P_{j}
.
This means that, in total,
P_{i}
has received valid signatures on
b
from
t+1
distinct parties. Among these, at least one party, say
P_{h}
, must be honest, as there can be at most
t
corrupt
parties. This means that
P_{h}
must have added
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{hj}
during some round
r^{\prime}
, where
r^{\prime}\leq t
.
Thus, as argued in the previous case, each party
P_{k}
also adds
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{kj}
by round
t+1
and hence time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
.

Lemma 23

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
achieves
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Validity in a synchronous network.

Proof

Suppose that all honest parties have the same input
b
. Corresponding to each
honest
party
P_{j}
, every
honest

P_{i}
sets
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}=\{b\}
. This is because
P_{i}
would receive a valid signature on
b
from
P_{j}
during round
0
, and adds
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
. Further,
P_{i}
will not add any
b^{\prime}\neq b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
during
any of the rounds, since the adversary
cannot
forge a signature on
b^{\prime}
on the behalf of
P_{j}
. Thus, for each honest
P_{j}
, party

P_{i}
adds
(j,b)
to
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
Hence corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, then value
(j,b)
is added to
\mathsf{FIN}_{i}
of every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
by time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
.
Moreover, since
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})

conditions holds,
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Consequently, as per the “majority” rule, every party in
\mathcal{H}
outputs
b
.

Lemma 24

In protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
, irrespective of the network type, all honest parties obtain an output at the time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
.

Proof

The proof follows from the fact that irrespective of the network type, the parties compute an output (which could be
\bot
) at the local time
(t+1)\cdot\Delta
.

If the inputs of the parties are of size
\ell
bits, then we invoke
\ell
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
. The following lemma describes the communication cost incurred while doing this.

Lemma 25

If the inputs of the parties are of size
\ell
bits, then
protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n^{4}\cdot\ell\cdot|\sigma|)
bits from the honest parties.

Proof

During round
0
, each party signs its input and sends this to every other party, incurring a total communication of

\mathcal{O}(\ell\cdot n^{2}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits. During the next
t
rounds,
each party
P_{i}
sends
(b,j,\mathsf{SET}_{j})
to every other party
P_{k}
at most
once
. This is because
P_{i}
sends this only if
b\notin\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
holds, and does not do this once it adds
b
to
\mathsf{ACC}_{ij}
. Considering all possibilities for
b
,
i
,
j
and
k
, and taking into account that
\mathsf{SET}_{j}
will contain
\mathcal{O}(n)
signatures, the communication cost of this will be
\mathcal{O}(\ell\cdot n^{4}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits.

The proof of Lemma
1
now follows from Lemma
22
-
25
.

0.A.2
Protocool
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
: Asynchronous Broadcast with Synchronous Guarantees

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
(see Fig
2
for the protocol description).

Lemma
2
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
achieves the following properties.

–

Asynchronous Network: The protocol is a
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-secure broadcast protocol.

–

Synchronous Network:
(a)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Liveness
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is honest, then all honest parties obtain an output
within time
3\Delta
.
(b)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Validity
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is honest, then every honest party with an output, outputs
m
.

(c)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Consistency
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is corrupt and some honest party outputs
m^{\star}
at time
T
,
then every honest
P_{i}
outputs
m^{\star}
by the end of time
T+\Delta
.

–

Communication Complexity:
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\ell\cdot|\sigma|)
bits are communicated by the honest parties, where

\ell
is the size of
\mathsf{Sen}
’s input.

Proof

We first consider a
synchronous
network, followed by an
asynchronous
network.

Properties in the Synchronous Network.

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
Suppose
\mathsf{Sen}
is
honest
. Then by time
\Delta
, each party in
\mathcal{H}
receives
\langle(\mathsf{propose},m)\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
from
\mathsf{Sen}

and no honest party ever receives
\langle(\mathsf{propose},m^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
from any party by time
2\Delta
, for any
m^{\prime}\neq m
, since signature of an
honest

\mathsf{Sen}

cannot
be forged.
Thus, every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}

sends
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m)\rangle_{j}
by time
2\Delta
.
Consequently, by time
3\Delta
, every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will have a quorum
\mathcal{C}(m)
of legitimately signed
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m)\rangle_{j}
messages corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
.
The parties
P_{k}\in Z^{\star}
may send signed
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m^{\prime})\rangle_{k}
messages where
m^{\prime}\neq m
and consequently
the parties in
\mathcal{H}
may also have a quorum
\mathcal{C}(m^{\prime})
of legitimately signed
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m^{\prime})\rangle_{k}
messages corresponding to every
P_{k}\in Z^{\star}
.
However, since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}=\mathcal{H}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Consequently, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
outputs
m
by time
3\Delta
, as the condition for outputting an
m^{\prime}\neq m
will be never satisfied for the parties in
\mathcal{H}
.
This proves the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-liveness and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity.

We now consider
\mathsf{Sen}
to be
corrupt
. We first show that no two parties in
\mathcal{H}
can vote for different messages. On the contrary,
let
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
sends
\langle\mathsf{vote},m^{\prime})\rangle_{i}
at time
T_{i}
, and let
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
sends
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m^{\prime\prime})\rangle_{j}
at time
T_{j}
,
where
T_{j}\geq T_{i}
. This implies that
P_{i}
must have received
\langle(\mathsf{propose},m^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
from
\mathsf{Sen}
within time
T_{i}-\Delta
, and would have sent

\langle(\mathsf{propose},m^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
to
P_{j}
. And
P_{j}
would have received
\langle(\mathsf{propose},m^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
from
P_{i}
within time
T_{i}
. Now since
T_{i}\leq T_{j}
,
it implies that
P_{j}

would not
have sent
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m^{\prime\prime})\rangle_{j}
at time
T_{j}
, and this is a contradiction.

Now based on the above fact, we proceed to prove that
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency holds.
Let
P_{h}\in\mathcal{H}
outputs
m^{\star}
at time
T
. This implies that at time
T
, there exists a subset
Z_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that

P_{h}
has a quorum
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star})
of legitimately signed
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m)\rangle_{j}
messages, corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus Z_{\alpha}
.
Now since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition, it follows that
\mathcal{H}\cap(\mathcal{P}\setminus Z_{\alpha})\neq\emptyset
. This implies that there exists at least
one party in
\mathcal{H}
, say
P_{k}
, who has voted for
m^{\star}
by sending a
\langle(\mathsf{vote},m)\rangle_{k}
message. Consequently, no other party in
\mathcal{H}
every votes for
m^{\star\star}\neq m^{\star}
.
The parties in
Z^{\star}
may vote for
m^{\star\star}\neq m^{\star}
. But since
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, it follows that no party in
\mathcal{H}
will ever have a sufficiently large
quorum of legitimately signed vote messages for
m^{\star\star}
to output
m^{\star\star}
.
Since
P_{h}
sends
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star})
to all parties at time
T
, every other party in
\mathcal{H}
will receive
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star})
by time
T+\Delta
.
Consequently, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will output
m^{\star}
, latest by time
T+\Delta
.

Properties in the Asynchronous Network.

We now consider an asynchronous network. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties. We first consider an
honest

\mathsf{Sen}
.
Each party in
\mathcal{H}
eventually receives
\langle(\mathsf{propose},m)\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
from
\mathsf{Sen}
. Furthermore, no party in
\mathcal{H}
ever receives
\langle(\mathsf{propose},m^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathsf{Sen}}
from any party,
for any
m^{\prime}\neq m
, since the signature of an honest
\mathsf{Sen}
cannot be forged. Hence, each party in
\mathcal{H}
eventually sends a signed
\mathsf{vote}
message for
m
, which is eventually
delivered to every party in
\mathcal{H}
. The parties in
Z^{\star}
may send signed
\mathsf{vote}
messages for
m^{\prime}\neq m
.
However, since
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(3)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition, it follows that each party in
\mathcal{H}
eventually outputs
m
and the conditions for outputting
m^{\prime}\neq m
will be never satisfied
for any party in
\mathcal{H}
. This proves the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-liveness and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency.

Next, consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{Sen}
.
Let
P_{h}\in\mathcal{H}
outputs
m^{\star}
. This implies there exists a subset
Z_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that

P_{h}
has a quorum
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star})
of legitimately signed
\mathsf{vote}
messages for
m^{\star}
, corresponding to
every party in
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z_{\alpha}
.
Now consider an
arbitrary

P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, where
P_{i}\neq P_{h}
. We claim that for any
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, party

P_{i}
will
never
have a
quorum
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star\star})
of legitimately signed
\mathsf{vote}
messages for
m^{\star\star}
, corresponding to
the parties in
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
.
On the contrary, let
P_{i}
eventually have a
quorum
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star\star})
of legitimately signed
\mathsf{vote}
messages for
m^{\star\star}
, corresponding to
every party in
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z_{\beta}
, for some
Z_{\beta}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Now since the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition is satisfied, it follows that
\mathcal{H}\cap(\mathcal{P}\setminus Z_{\alpha})\cap(\mathcal{P}\setminus Z_{\beta})\neq\emptyset
.
This implies that there exists at least one party from
\mathcal{H}
, say
P_{k}
, such that
P_{k}
has voted
both
for
m^{\star}
, as well as
m^{\star\star}
, which is a
contradiction. Consequently,
P_{i}
will never output
m^{\star\star}
.
Now, since
P_{h}
sends
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star})
to all the parties, party
P_{i}
eventually receives
\mathcal{C}(m^{\star})
and outputs
m^{\star}
.
This proves
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency.

Finally, the communication complexity follows from the fact that irrespective of the type of network, every party may have to send a quorum of up to
\mathcal{O}(n)
signed
\mathsf{vote}

messages, to every other party.

0.A.3
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
(see Fig
3
for the formal description).

Theorem
3.2
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
achieves the following,
with a communication complexity of
\mathcal{O}(n^{4}\cdot\ell\cdot|\sigma|)
bits,
where
T_{\mathsf{BC}}=3\Delta+T_{\mathsf{PW}}
.

–

Synchronous network:

–

(a)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Liveness
: At time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, each honest party has an output.

–

(b)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Validity
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is honest, then at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, each honest party
outputs
m
.

–

(c)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Consistency
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is corrupt, then the output of every honest party is the same at
time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.

–

(d)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Fallback Consistency
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is corrupt and some honest
party outputs
m^{\star}\neq\bot
at time

T
through fallback-mode, then every honest party outputs
m^{\star}
by
time
T+\Delta
.

–

Asynchronous Network:

–

(a)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Liveness
: At time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, each honest party has an output.

–

(b)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Weak Validity
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is honest, then at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
each honest party
outputs
m
or
\bot
.

–

(c)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Fallback Validity
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is honest, then each honest party
with output
\bot
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, eventually outputs

m
through fallback-mode.

–

(d)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Weak Consistency
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is corrupt, then there exists some
m^{\star}\neq\bot
, such that
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
each honest party
outputs either

m^{\star}
or
\bot
.

–

(e)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Fallback Consistency
: If
\mathsf{Sen}
is corrupt, and some honest party
outputs
m^{\star}\neq\bot
at time

T
where
T\geq T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, then
each honest party eventually outputs
m^{\star}
.

Proof

The
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-liveness and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-liveness properties follow from the fact that
every honest party outputs something (including
\bot
) at (local)
time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, irrespective of the type of the network.
We next prove the rest of the properties of the protocol in the
synchronous
network.

Properties in the Synchronous Network.

If
\mathsf{Sen}
is
honest
, then due to the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-liveness
and

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Lemma
2
), all honest
parties receive
m
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}

at time
3\Delta
. Consequently, all honest parties participate with input
m
in the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
.
The
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-guaranteed liveness
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
properties
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Lemma
1
) guarantees that at time
3\Delta+T_{\mathsf{PW}}
, all
honest parties will have

m
as the output from the instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
. As a result, all honest parties output
m
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, thus proving the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
property.

To prove the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
property, we consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{Sen}
.
From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
property of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Lemma
1
),
all honest parties will have the
same
output from the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
If
all
honest parties have the output
\bot
for
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, then
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency

holds trivially. So, consider the case when
some
honest
party, say
P_{i}
, has the output
m^{\star}\neq\bot
for
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
This implies that all honest parties have the output
m^{\star}
from the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
.
Moreover, at time
3\Delta
, at least one
honest
party, say
P_{h}
, has received
m^{\star}
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
.
If the latter does not hold, then all honest parties would have participated
with input
\bot
in the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
, and from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}

in the
synchronous

network (see Lemma
1
), all honest parties would compute
\bot
as the output during the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
, which is a contradiction.
Since
P_{h}
has received

m^{\star}
from
\mathsf{Sen}
’s Acast at time
3\Delta
, it follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
property of

\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the
synchronous

network (see Lemma
2
) that
all
honest parties will receive

m^{\star}
from
\mathsf{Sen}
’s Acast by time
4\Delta
. Moreover,
4\Delta<3\Delta+T_{\mathsf{PW}}
holds.
Consequently, at time
3\Delta+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,

all
honest parties will have
m^{\star}
from
\mathsf{Sen}
’s Acast
and
as the output of

\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
, implying that all honest parties output
m^{\star}
for
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
.

We next prove the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-fallback consistency
property for which
we again consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{Sen}
.
Let
P_{h}
be an
honest
party who outputs
m^{\star}\neq\bot
at time
T
through fallback-mode.
Note that
T>T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, as the output during the fallback-mode is computed only after time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
We also note that
each
honest party has output
\bot
at
time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. This is because, from the proof of the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
property of

\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
(see above), if any
honest
party has an output

m^{\prime}\neq\bot
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, then
all
honest parties (including
P_{h}
) must have computed the output

m^{\prime}
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. Hence,
P_{h}
will never change its output to

m^{\star}
.
19
19

19

Recall that in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
, the parties who obtain
an output different from
\bot
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, never change their output.

Now since
P_{h}
has obtained the output
m^{\star}
, it implies that at time
T
, it has received
m^{\star}
from the Acast of

\mathsf{Sen}
. It then follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
of

\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the
synchronous
network that every honest party will also receive

m^{\star}
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
, latest by time
T+\Delta
and output
m^{\star}
.
This completes the proof of all the properties in the
synchronous
network.

Properties in the Asynchronous Network.

The
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity
property follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
property of

\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the
asynchronous
network (see Lemma
2
), which ensures that
no honest party ever receives an
m^{\prime}
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
, where
m^{\prime}\neq m
.
So, if at all any honest party outputs a value different from
\bot
at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, it has to be
m
.
The
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak consistency
property follows using similar arguments
as used to prove
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
in the
synchronous
network;
however we now rely on the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
and

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency

properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the asynchronous network (see Lemma
2
).
The latter property ensures that
for a
corrupt

\mathsf{Sen}
, two different honest parties never end up receiving
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
, where
m_{1}\neq m_{2}
.

For the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-fallback validity
property, consider an
honest

\mathsf{Sen}
, and let

P_{i}
be an arbitrary
honest
party
who outputs
\bot
at (local) time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. Since the parties keep on participating in the protocol beyond time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
it follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-liveness
and

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the
asynchronous
network (see Lemma
2
) that party
P_{i}
will
eventually

receive
m
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
, by executing the steps of the fallback-mode of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Consequently, party
P_{i}
eventually changes its output from

\bot
to
m
.

For the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback consistency
property, we consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{Sen}
. Let

P_{j}
be an
honest
party who outputs some
m^{\star}
different from
\bot
at time
T
, where
T\geq T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
This implies that
P_{j}
has
obtained
m^{\star}
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
.
Now, consider an arbitrary
honest

P_{i}
. From the

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-liveness
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak consistency
properties of

\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in
asynchronous
network proved above,
it follows that
P_{i}
outputs either
m^{\star}
or
\bot
at local time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
If
P_{i}
has output
\bot
, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency

property of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
in the
asynchronous
network (see Lemma
2
), it follows that

P_{i}
will also eventually obtain
m^{\star}
from the Acast of
\mathsf{Sen}
, by executing the steps of the fallback-mode of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Consequently, party
P_{i}
eventually changes its output from

\bot
to
m^{\star}
.

The
communication complexity
(both in the synchronous as well as asynchronous network)
follows from the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{PW}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Acast}}
.

0.A.4
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{SBA}}

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{SBA}}
(see Fig
4
for the formal description).

Theorem
3.3
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{SBA}}
achieves the following where
T_{\mathsf{SBA}}=T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
incurring a communication of

\mathcal{O}(n^{5}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits.

–

Synchronous Network: the protocol is a
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-secure SBA protocol where honest parties have an output, different from
\bot
, at time
T_{\mathsf{SBA}}
.

–

Asynchronous Network: the protocol achieves
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-guaranteed liveness and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity, such that all honest parties have an output at (local) time
T_{\mathsf{SBA}}
.

Proof

The communication complexity simply follows from the fact that
n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
are invoked in the protocol.
The
guaranteed liveness
, both in the synchronous and asynchronous network trivially follows from
the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-liveness
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-liveness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
(see Theorem
3.2
), which ensures that all the
n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
produce
some
output
within (local) time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, both in a synchronous as well as an asynchronous network, through
regular
mode.
Hence, at local time
T_{\mathsf{SBA}}=T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, all honest parties will have some output.
We next prove the rest of the properties in a
synchronous
network.

Properties in the Synchronous Network.

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
In a
synchronous
network, the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
corresponding to the senders in
\mathcal{H}
,
result in an output different from
\bot
for all honest parties (follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
property of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous

network, Theorem
3.2
). Hence
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{SV}
will hold.
Moreover,
from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
property of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
),
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
obtain a common output from the
i^{th}
instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Hence, all honest parties output the
same
value,
different from
\bot
, at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, proving the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{SBA}}
.
Finally, if all parties in
\mathcal{H}
have the same input bit
b
, then only the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
corresponding to the parties in
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{H}
may output

\bar{b}=1-b
. However,
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{SV}\setminus Z^{\star}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
(since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition).
It then follows that all honest parties output
b
, proving
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{SBA}}
.

Properties in the Asynchronous Network.

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
Suppose all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
have the same input bit
b
. Let

P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
be an
arbitrary
party, that obtains an output
c
,
different
from
\bot
, at time
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. This implies that there exists a subset of parties
\mathcal{SV}
for
P_{i}
, where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{SV}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that

P_{i}
has obtained a Boolean output
b_{i}^{(j)}
from the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
instances, corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{SV}
. Moreover, there also exists a subset of parties

\mathcal{SV}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{SV}
, where
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{SV}_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that
the output
b_{i}^{(j)}=c
, corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{SV}_{i}
. Now since the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})

condition is satisfied, it follows that
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{SV}_{i},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition and hence

\mathcal{SV}_{i}\cap\mathcal{H}\neq\emptyset
. Consequently,
c=b
holds.
This proves the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity
in the asynchronous network.

0.A.5
Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
: Asynchronous Graded Agreement with Synchronous Validity

To design protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
, we first design a sub-protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
for proposing values.

0.A.5.1
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
: A Network Agnostic Protocol for Proposing Values

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
takes an input value from each party from the set
\{0,1,\lambda\}

and outputs a set
\mathsf{prop}
of proposed values for each party. Liveness is ensured in an
asynchronous
network as long as each honest party holds one of
two
inputs. In an asynchronous network, it will be ensured that each value in the output
\mathsf{prop}
must be the input of some
honest
party. Moreover, if any two honest parties output a singleton set for
\mathsf{prop}
, then they must output the
same
set. In a
synchronous
network, validity and liveness are ensured as long as each honest party participates with the same input. Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
is presented in figure
16
.

\justify

1.

Set
\mathsf{vals}=\mathsf{prop}=\emptyset
.

2.

On having the input
v\in\{0,1,\lambda\}
, send
(\mathsf{prepare},v)
to every party
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
.

3.

On receiving
(\mathsf{prepare},b)
for some
b\in\{0,1,\lambda\}
from a set of parties
S^{(b)}
satisfying
\mathbb{Q}^{1}(S^{(b)},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
send
(\mathsf{prepare},b)
to all
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
, if not sent earlier.

4.

Upon receiving the message
(\mathsf{prepare},b)
for some
b\in\{0,1,\lambda\}
from parties in set
\mathsf{PrepareSet}^{(b)}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, set
\mathsf{vals}=\mathsf{vals}\cup\{b\}
.

5.

Upon adding the first value
b
to
\mathsf{vals}
, send
(\mathsf{propose},b)
to every party
P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}
.

6.

Upon receiving
(\mathsf{propose},b)
messages from a set of parties
\mathsf{ProposeSet}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
on values
b\in\mathsf{vals}
, let

\mathsf{prop}\subseteq\mathsf{vals}
be the set of values carried by those messages.
Output
\mathsf{prop}
.

Figure 16
:
Sub-protocol to propose values. The above code is executed by every party
P_{i}
with input
v\in\{0,1,\lambda\}

The guarantees provided by
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
are proven in a series of lemmas below. In the below proofs, we assume that
Z^{\star}
is the set of corrupt parties.

Lemma 26

Suppose that the network is asynchronous. If two honest parties
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
output
\{b\}
and
\{b^{\prime}\}
respectively, then
b=b^{\prime}
.

Proof

Since
P_{i}
outputs
\{b\}
, it must have received
(\mathsf{propose},b)
from a set of parties
\mathsf{ProposeSet}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Similarly, since
P_{j}
outputs
\{b^{\prime}\},it
must have received
(\mathsf{propose},b^{\prime})
from a set of parties
\mathsf{ProposeSet}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\prime}
for some
Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Let
\mathsf{ProposeSet}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathsf{ProposeSet}_{\mathcal{H}}
be the set of
honest
parties in
\mathsf{ProposeSet}
and
\mathsf{ProposeSet}^{\prime}
respectively. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})

condition and
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
, it follows
that
\mathsf{ProposeSet}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap\mathsf{ProposeSet}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}\neq\emptyset
. Let
P_{h}\in\mathsf{ProposeSet}_{\mathcal{H}}\cap\mathsf{ProposeSet}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
If
b\neq b^{\prime}
, this would mean that
P_{h}
has sent both
(\mathsf{propose},b)
and
(\mathsf{propose},b^{\prime})
, which is a contradiction, since an honest party sends

at most
one
\mathsf{propose}
message as per the protocol.

Lemma 27

Suppose that the network is asynchronous. If no honest party has input
v
, then no honest party outputs
\mathsf{prop}
containing
v
.

Proof

If
v
was not input by any honest party, then no honest party sends
(\mathsf{prepare},v)
in step
2
. Hence, no honest party receives
(\mathsf{prepare},v)
from a set of parties
S^{(v)}

which satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{1}(S^{(v)},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition during step
3
, since such a set must contain at least one honest party. Consequently, no honest party sends
(\mathsf{propose},v)
. Thus, no honest party adds
v
to
\mathsf{vals}
and no honest party outputs
\mathsf{prop}
containing
v
.

The following lemmas help prove liveness.

Lemma 28

Suppose that the network is asynchronous. If all honest parties hold one of two inputs, say
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
, then all honest parties eventually compute
an output.

Proof

We first show that every honest party eventually sends a
\mathsf{propose}
message.
Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be the sets of
honest
parties holding inputs
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
respectively
and let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of
honest
parties.
We know that due to the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition,
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the

\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition. Now, consider the following cases.

–

Case 1 -
\mathcal{H}_{0}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition
: In this case,
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is a candidate for the set
S^{(v_{0})}
, since all
the parties in
\mathcal{H}_{0}
send
(\mathsf{prepare},v_{0})
in step
2
.

–

Case 2 -
\mathcal{H}_{0}
does not satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition
: In this case,
\mathcal{Z}_{s}

must satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition, since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the

\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition. Then similar to what was argued in the previous case,
\mathcal{H}_{1}
is a candidate for the set
S^{(v_{1})}
.

Let
v_{b}
be the input corresponding to the candidate set
S^{(v_{b})}
. Every honest party will now eventually send
(\mathsf{prepare},v_{b})
in step
3
.
This would mean that the set of honest parties
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
is a candidate for the set
\mathsf{PrepareSet}^{(v_{b})}
.
Thus, every honest party eventually adds
v_{b}
to
\mathsf{vals}
and sends a
\mathsf{propose}
message for some value
v_{b}
.
This way, every honest party eventually receives
\mathsf{propose}
messages from every other honest party for
v_{b}
