and thus, the set
\mathcal{H}
also forms a candidate for the set
\mathsf{ProposeSet}
. Thus, all
honest parties eventually compute an output.

Lemma 29

If the network is synchronous and if all honest parties participate with input
v
, then all honest parties output
\mathsf{prop}=\{v\}
at time
2\Delta
.

Proof

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties. Then
in step
2
, every party in
\mathcal{H}
sends
(\mathsf{prepare},v)
to every party, which is delivered within
\Delta
time.
Thus, at time
\Delta
, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
receive
(\mathsf{prepare},v)
from the parties in
\mathcal{H}
and add
v
to
\mathsf{vals}
. Further, no other value
v^{\star}
will be
added to
\mathsf{vals}
, since only the parties in
Z^{\star}
may send a
\mathsf{prepare}
message for
v^{\star}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}

does not
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(Z^{\star},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition.
Thus, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
send
(\mathsf{propose},v)
in step
5
, which gets delivered to all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
at time
2\Delta
.
Consequently, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
output
\mathsf{prop}=\{v\}
.

Lemma 30

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
bits.

Proof

The proof follows from the fact that each party sends
(\mathsf{prepare},b)
to every other party at most once for any value of
b\in\{0,1,\lambda\}
.

0.A.5.2
The Graded Agreement Protocol

We now present protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
(Figure
17
) based on protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
. The protocol cleverly “stitches” together two instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
, by defining the input for the second instance based on the output from the first instance. Each party, with input either
0
or
1
, participates in the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
with their input. Since the parties participate with one of two inputs, this instance will
eventually complete (in an
asynchronous
network),
and the parties obtain an output, say
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
. Only if
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
is a singleton set, say
\{b\}
, for some party, then that party participates in the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
with the input
b
. Otherwise, it participates in the second instance with a
default
input of
\lambda
. Since no two honest parties can output different singleton sets for
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
, this ensures that each honest party participates with an input of either
b
or

\lambda
(in an
asynchronous
network).
Thus, the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
also eventually completed with an output, say
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
. This also ensures that
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
can contain only values
b
and
\lambda
. If
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
contains
only

b
for some honest party, then that party outputs
b
with a grade of
2
. If
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
contains
b
along with
\lambda
, then the party outputs
b
with a grade of
1
.
Else, if
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
contains only
\lambda
, then the party outputs
\bot
with a grade of
0
. If the network is
synchronous
and all honest parties start the protocol with the same input, then

both

\mathsf{prop}_{1}

as well as
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
will be a
singleton
set containing that value and hence all honest parties will output that value with the highest grade.

\justify

1.

On having the input
v\in\{0,1\}
, set
b_{1}=v
. Participate in an instance of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
with input
b_{1}
and wait for its completion. Let

\mathsf{prop}_{1}
be the output computed during the instance of
\mathsf{prop}
.

2.

If
\mathsf{prop}_{1}=\{b\}
for some
b\in\{0,1\}
, then set
b_{2}=b
. Else, set
b_{2}=\lambda
. Then
participate in an instance of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
with input
b_{2}
and wait for its completion. Let
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
be the output computed from this instance of
\mathsf{prop}
.

3.

If
\mathsf{prop}_{2}=\{b^{\prime}\}
and
b^{\prime}\neq\lambda
, then output
(b^{\prime},2)
. If
\mathsf{prop}_{2}=\{b^{\prime},\lambda\}
where
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
, then output
(b^{\prime},1)
. Else, if
\mathsf{prop}_{2}=\{\lambda\}
, then
output
(\bot,0)
.

Figure 17
:
Asynchronous graded agreement with synchronous validity. The above code is executed by every party
P_{i}
with input
v\in\{0,1\}
.

We now proceed to prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
.

Lemma 31

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
achieves the following in a synchronous network, where
T_{\mathsf{GA}}=4\Delta
.

–

(a)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Liveness
:
If all honest parties participate in the protocol with the same input, then at time
T_{\mathsf{GA}}
,
all honest parties obtain an output.

–

(b)
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Graded Validity
: If every honest party’s input is
b
, then all honest parties with an output, output
(b,2)
.

Proof

If all honest parties participate in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
with the same input
b
, then
from Lemma
29
, all honest parties output
\mathsf{prop}_{1}=\{b\}
at time
2\Delta
.
Thus, all honest parties participate with input
b_{2}=b
in the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
and,
once again from Lemma
29
, output
\mathsf{prop}_{2}=\{b\}
at time
4\Delta
. Thus, all honest parties output
(b,2)
.

Lemma 32

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
achieves the following in an asynchronous network.

–

(a)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Liveness
: If all honest parties participate in the protocol with a binary input, then each honest party eventually obtains an output.

–

(b)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Validity
: If every honest party’s input is
b
, then all honest parties with an output, output
(b,2)
.

–

(c)
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Consistency
: If two honest parties output grades
g,g^{\prime}
, then
|g-g^{\prime}|\leq 1
holds; moreover,
if two honest parties output
(v,g)
and
(v^{\prime},g^{\prime})
with
g,g^{\prime}\geq 1
, then
v=v^{\prime}
.

Proof

Since each honest party participates with a binary input,
from Lemma
28
, each party eventually outputs some value for
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
during the first instance of
\mathsf{prop}
. Now there are two possible cases.

–

Case 1 - Some honest party outputs
\{b\}
as its value for
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
where
b\in\{0,1\}
:
From Lemma
26
, no honest party can output
\{b^{\prime}\}
, where
b\neq b^{\prime}
, as
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
.
Thus, each honest party participates with input either
b
or
\lambda
for the second instance of
\mathsf{prop}
.

–

Case 2 - No honest party outputs
\{b\}
as its value for
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
for any
b\in\{0,1\}
: In this case, all honest parties participate with input
\lambda
in the second instance of
\mathsf{prop}
.

In either case, the honest parties participate in the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
with no more than
two
different inputs.
Thus, from Lemma
28
, all parties eventually compute some value for
\mathsf{prop}_{2}
during the second instance of
\mathsf{prop}
and hence compute some
output for protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
. This proves the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Liveness.

We next prove the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Consistency. We first show that the grades output by any two parties differ by at most
1
. For this,
suppose that some
honest
party
P_{i}
outputs
(b,2)
. We show that no other honest party
P_{j}
can output
(\bot,0)
.
Since
P_{i}
output
(b,2)
, from Lemma
26
,
P_{j}
cannot output
\mathsf{prop}_{2}=\{\lambda\}
. Thus,
P_{j}
cannot output
(\bot,0)
.
Next, we show that any two
honest
parties which output
non-zero
grades must output the same value. Similar to what was argued for the proof of
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Liveness,
there exists a bit
b
such that each honest party participates in
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
with input
b
or
\lambda
during step
2
.
Thus,
\mathsf{prop}_{2}\subseteq\{b,\lambda\}
for every honest party. This means that any honest party which outputs a non-zero grade must output it along with the bit
b
.

We finally prove the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Validity. Suppose that each honest party participates with the same input bit
b
.
From Lemma
28
, we know that all honest parties output some value for
\mathsf{prop}_{1}
. From Lemma
27
, all honest parties must output
\mathsf{prop}_{1}=\{b\}
.
Hence, all honest parties participate in
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
in step
2
with input
b
. By the same argument, all honest parties output
\mathsf{prop}_{2}=\{b\}
. Hence, all honest parties output
(b,2)
.

Lemma 33

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
bits.

Proof

The proof follows from Lemma
30
, since
\Pi_{\mathsf{Prop}}
is invoked twice in the protocol.

0.A.6
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
(see Fig
5
for the protocol steps). We start with the properties in the
asynchronous
network first, which mostly follows from
[
23
]

and are recalled from
[
23
]
.
We start with the validity property.

Lemma 34

In protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
, if the network is asynchronous and all honest parties have the same input bit
b
, then
all honest parties eventually output
b
.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties.
If every honest party has the same input bit
b
,
then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
32
),
all honest parties eventually output
(b,2)
at the end of the first as well as the second instance of the
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
protocol
during the first iteration. Consequently, every
honest
party eventually sends a signed
(\mathsf{ready},b)
message to all the parties and only the parties
in
Z^{\star}
may send a signed
(\mathsf{ready},\overline{b})
message. It now follows easily from the steps of the output computation stage that
no
honest party ever
sends a signed
(\mathsf{ready},\overline{b})
message and all hence honest parties eventually output
b
.

We next prove the
consistency
property.

Lemma 35

In protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
, if the network is asynchronous and if any honest party outputs
b
, then
every other honest party eventually outputs
b
.

Proof

Let
P_{i}
be the
first

honest
party who sends a signed
\mathsf{ready}
message for some bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, during some iteration, say iteration
r
.
We show that
no
honest party ever sends a signed
(\mathsf{ready},\overline{b})
message during iteration
r
or in the subsequent iterations.
Since
P_{i}
has sent a signed
\mathsf{ready}
message for
b
, it implies that
P_{i}
outputs
(b,2)
in the second instance of the
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
protocol during iteration
r
and sets
\mathsf{committed}
to
\mathsf{true}
.
Then, from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Consistency
of

\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
32
),
every other honest party
outputs either
(b,2)
or
(b,1)
in the second instance of the
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
protocol during iteration
r
. Consequently, no other
honest party sends the signed
(\mathsf{ready},\overline{b})
message during iteration
r
. Also, from the protocol steps, all honest parties
update their input
to
b
for the next iteration. This further implies that all honest parties will continue to input
b

to each subsequent invocation of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
, ignoring the output of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
, for as long as they continue running.
Consequently, no honest party ever sends a signed
(\mathsf{ready},\overline{b})
message.

Now let some
honest
party, say
P_{h}
, computes the output
b
during iteration
k
. This
implies that
P_{h}
receives the signed
(\mathsf{ready},b)
message from a set of parties, say
{\mathcal{}T}
, such that

\mathcal{P}\setminus{\mathcal{}T}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. The set
{\mathcal{}T}
is bound to have at least one
honest
party, due to the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition, implying that
at least one honest party has sent a signed
(\mathsf{ready},b)
message, either during the iteration
k
or some previous iteration.
From the protocol steps,
P_{h}
sends
\mathcal{C}(b)
, the set of signed
(\mathsf{ready},b)
messages of the parties in
{\mathcal{}T}
to all other parties, which get eventually delivered.
Moreover, as shown above,
no honest party will ever send a signed
(\mathsf{ready},\overline{b})
message.
Consequently, every honest party
eventually receives sufficiently many numbers of signed
(\mathsf{ready},b)
messages
and outputs
b
.

We next prove that at the end of each iteration, the updated value of all honest parties will be the same with the probability
at least
\frac{1}{2n}
.

Lemma 36

In protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
, if the network is asynchronous and if
all honest parties participate during iteration
k
, then with probability at least
\frac{1}{2n}
, all honest parties have the same updated bit

b
at the end of iteration
k
.

Proof

To prove the lemma statement, we consider an event
\mathsf{Agree}
, which denotes that all honest parties have the same input for the second instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
during iteration
k
. If the event
\mathsf{Agree}
occurs, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
asynchronous

network (Lemma
32
),
all honest parties will have the same
updated bit at the end of iteration
k
. We show that the event
\mathsf{Agree}
occurs during iteration
k
with a probability of at least
\frac{1}{2n}
.
For this, we consider two different possible cases with respect to the output from the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
during iteration
k
.

–

Case I: No honest party obtains an output
(b,2)
for any
b\in\{0,1\}
during the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
.

In this case, all honest parties set the output from the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
during iteration
k
as the input for the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
.
From the
(\mathcal{Z}_{a},p)
-commonness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
in
asynchronous
network
[
23
]
,
all honest parties will have the same output bit
\mathsf{Coin}_{k}
from the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
with a probability of at least

p=\frac{1}{n}>\frac{1}{2n}
.

–

Case II: Some honest party obtains an output
(b,2)
during the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
.

In this case, the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Graded Consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
asynchronous

network (Lemma
32
)
ensure that all honest parties obtain the output

(b,2)
or
(b,1)
from the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
. Moreover, from the protocol steps, the output of the
instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
during iteration
k
is
not
revealed, until the first honest party generates an output from the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
during iteration

k
. Consequently, the output bit
b
from the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
is
independent
of the output of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
.
From the
(\mathcal{Z}_{a},p)
-commonness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
in
asynchronous
network
[
23
]
,
all honest parties will have the same output bit
\mathsf{Coin}_{k}
from the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
with a probability of at least

p=\frac{1}{n}
. Then the probability that
\mathsf{Coin}_{k}=b
holds is at least
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{2n}
and with this probability,
all honest
parties will have the same input for the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
.

We next derive the expected number of iterations required in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
for the honest parties to produce an output.
This automatically gives the expected running time in an asynchronous network, since each iteration takes a constant time.

Lemma 37

If the network is asynchronous, then
in protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
, it requires expected
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})

iterations for the honest parties to compute an output.

Proof

To prove the lemma, we need to derive the expected number of iterations, until all the honest parties have the same input during the second instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
of an iteration. This is because once all the honest parties have the same input during the second instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
of an iteration, then all honest parties will set
\mathsf{committed}
to
\mathsf{true}
at the end of that iteration and start sending signed
\mathsf{ready}
messages, followed
by computing an output.
Let
\tau
be the random variable
which counts the number of iterations until all honest parties
have the same input during the second instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in an iteration.
Then the probability that
\tau=k
is given as:

\displaystyle\Pr(\tau=k)

\displaystyle=\Pr(\tau\neq 1)\cdot\Pr(\tau\neq 2\mid\tau\neq 1)\cdot\ldots\cdot Pr(\tau\neq(k-1)\mid\tau\neq 1\cap\ldots\cap

\displaystyle\tau\neq(k-2))\cdot\Pr(\tau=k\mid\tau\neq 1\cap\ldots\cap\tau\neq(k-1)).

From Lemma
36
,
every multiplicand on the right-hand side in
the above equation, except the last one, is upper bounded by
(1-\frac{1}{2n})

and the last multiplicand is upper bounded by
\frac{1}{2n}
. Hence, we get

\Pr(\tau=k)\leq(1-\frac{1}{2n})^{k-1}(\frac{1}{2n}).

Now the expected
value
E(\tau)
of
\tau
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle E(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\tau\cdot\Pr(\tau=k)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k(1-\frac{1}{2n})^{k-1}(\frac{1}{2n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k(1-\frac{1}{2n})^{k-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-(1-\frac{1}{2n})}+\frac{1-\frac{1}{2n}}{\Big(1-(1-\frac{1}{2n})\Big)^{2}}

\displaystyle=2n+4n^{2}-2n=4n^{2}

The expression for
E(\tau)
is a sum of
AGP
up to infinite terms, which is
given by
\frac{a}{1-r}+\frac{dr}{(1-r)^{2}}
, where
a=1
,
r=1-\frac{1}{2n}
and
d=1
. Hence, we have
E(\tau)\leq 4n^{2}
.

We finally prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
in a
synchronous
network.

Lemma 38

If the network is synchronous and if all honest parties have the same input
b\in\{0,1\}
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{ABA}}
, then all honest parties output
b
,
at time
T_{\mathsf{ABA}}=T_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}+2T_{\mathsf{GA}}+\Delta
.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
If all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
participate with input
b
, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-liveness

and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Graded Validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
synchronous
network (Lemma
31
), all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
output

(b,2)
during the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
at time
T_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the first iteration. The
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Guaranteed Liveness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
synchronous
network
[
23
]

ensures that all honest parties compute some output from the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
during the first iteration at the time
T_{\mathsf{GA}}+T_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
.
Since the parties in
\mathcal{H}
output
(b,2)
during the first instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
, they participate with input
b
during the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
.
Consequently, from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-liveness

and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Graded Validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the
synchronous
network,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
compute the output
(b,2)
during the second instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
in the first iteration at the time
2T_{\mathsf{GA}}+T_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
.
Hence every party in
\mathcal{H}
sends a signed
\mathsf{ready}
message for
b
at the time
2T_{\mathsf{GA}}+T_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
, which gets delivered at the time
T_{\mathsf{ABA}}
.
Moreover, only the parties in
Z^{\star}
may send a signed
\mathsf{ready}
message for
\overline{b}
.
Since
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{H}=Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and since
\mathcal{H}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
(due to the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition),
it follows that all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have sufficiently many signed
\mathsf{ready}
messages for
b
at the time
T_{\mathsf{ABA}}
to output
b
.

The proof of Theorem
3.4
now follows from Lemma
34
-
38
.
The communication complexity follows from the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}

[
23
]
and the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
(Lemma
33
)
and the fact that in a
synchronous
network, only a
constant
number of invocations of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
are involved, while in an
asynchronous
network,
there are
\mbox{poly}(n)
invocations of
\Pi_{\mathsf{GA}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{CoinFlip}}
in
expectation
.

Appendix 0.B
Properties of Our Network Agnostic ICP

In this section, we prove the properties of our network-agnostic ICP (see Fig
6
for the formal details). Throughout this section, we assume that
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the conditions

\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
,
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
,
\mathbb{Q}^{(3)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
and
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
.

Lemma 39

If
\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I}
and
\mathsf{R}
are honest, then the following hold during protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Correctness
: In a synchronous network, each honest party sets
\mathsf{authCompleted}_{(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R})}

to
1
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
at time
T_{\mathsf{Auth}}=\Delta+4T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. Moreover
\mathsf{R}
outputs
s
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
which takes
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}=\Delta
time.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Correctness
: In an asynchronous network, each honest
party eventually sets
\mathsf{authCompleted}_{(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R})}

to
1
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
and
\mathsf{R}
eventually outputs
s
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.

Proof

We first start with the
synchronous
network. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be set of
corrupt
parties and let

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
,
\mathsf{S}
chooses a random
t
-degree signing-polynomial
F(x)
such that
s=F(0)
holds,
a random
t
-degree masking-polynomial
M(x)
, and computes
verification points
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
such that
v_{i}=F(\alpha_{i})
and
m_{i}=M(\alpha_{i})
hold.
\mathsf{S}
then
sends the signing-polynomial
F(x)
and masking-polynomial
M(x)
to
\mathsf{I}
, and the
corresponding verification-point
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
to each verifier
P_{i}
.
Consequently, each verifier in
\mathcal{H}

receives its verification-point by time
\Delta
, and indicates this by broadcasting
(\mathsf{Received},i)
. Since
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{H}=Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
,
from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
),
it follows that at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
\mathsf{S}
will find a set
\mathcal{SV}
, such that
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{SV}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, where
each verifier in
\mathcal{SV}
has indicated that it has received its verification-point. Consequently,
\mathsf{S}
will broadcast
\mathcal{SV}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network,
\mathsf{I}
will receive
\mathcal{SV}
at time
\Delta+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Moreover, due to the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network,
party
\mathsf{I}
would have gotten
(\mathsf{Received},i)
, corresponding to every verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{SV}
, by time
\Delta+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{SV}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
will hold.
Hence,
\mathsf{I}
will randomly select
d\in\mathbb{F}
, compute
B(x)=dF(x)+M(x)
, and broadcast
(d,B(x))
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network,
this will be delivered to every honest party, including

\mathsf{S}
, by time
\Delta+3T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{S}
will find that
B(\alpha_{j})=dv_{j}+m_{j}
holds
for all the verifiers
P_{j}\in\mathcal{SV}
. Consequently,
\mathsf{S}
will broadcast an
\mathsf{OK}
message, which is received by every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
at time
\Delta+4T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, due to the

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network.
Thus, each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
sets
\mathsf{authCompleted}_{(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R})}

to
1
, while
\mathsf{I}
additionally sets
\mathsf{ICSig}(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R},s)
to
F(x)
at time
\Delta+4T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.

During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
,
\mathsf{I}
will send
F(x)
to
\mathsf{R}
, and each verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV}
will send its
verification point
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
to
\mathsf{R}
. These points and the polynomial
F(x)
are received by

\mathsf{R}
within
\Delta
time. Moreover, the condition
v_{i}=F(\alpha_{i})
will hold true for these points, and consequently, these points
will be
accepted
.
Since
\mathcal{SV}\setminus(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV})\subseteq Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, it follows that at time
\Delta
, receiver

\mathsf{R}
will find a subset
\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{SV}
where
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that the points corresponding to
all the parties in
\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}
are accepted.
This implies that
\mathsf{R}
will output
s=F(0)
within time
\Delta
.

The proof for the asynchronous case is similar as above, except that each “favourable” event occurs
eventually
, and follows from the fact that every set in
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
is a subset of some set in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Moreover, we rely on the
properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network.

We next prove the privacy property, for which we again need to consider an
honest

\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I}
and
\mathsf{R}
.

Lemma 40

If
\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I}
and
\mathsf{R}
are honest, then the view of
\mathsf{Adv}
remains independent of
s
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, irrespective of the network
type.

Proof

We prove privacy in a
synchronous
network. The privacy in an
asynchronous
network automatically follows, since
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
is a subset of
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Let
t=\max\{|Z|:Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}\}
and let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of corrupt parties.
For simplicity and without loss of generality, let

|Z^{\star}|=t
.
During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, the adversary
\mathsf{Adv}
learns
t
verification-points
\{(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})\}_{P_{i}\in Z^{\star}}
.
However, since
F(x)
is a random
t
-degree polynomial with
F(0)=s
,
the points
\{(\alpha_{i},v_{i})\}_{P_{i}\in Z^{\star}}
are distributed independently of
s
.
That is, for every candidate
s\in\mathbb{F}
from the point of view of
\mathsf{Adv}
, there is a corresponding unique

t
-degree polynomial
F(x)
, such that
F(\alpha_{i})=v_{i}
holds corresponding to every
P_{i}\in Z^{\star}
.

During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, the adversary
\mathsf{Adv}
also learns
d
and the blinded-polynomial
B(x)=dF(x)+M(x)
.
However, this does not add any new information about
s
to the view of the adversary. This is because

M(x)
is a random
t
-degree polynomial and
\mathsf{Adv}
learns
t
points on
M(x)
, corresponding to the parties in
Z^{\star}
.
Hence, for every candidate
M(x)
polynomial from the point of view of

\mathsf{Adv}
where
M(\alpha_{i})=m_{i}
holds for every
P_{i}\in Z^{\star}
, there is a corresponding unique

t
-degree polynomial
F(x)
, such that
F(\alpha_{i})=v_{i}
holds corresponding to every
P_{i}\in Z^{\star}
, and where

dF(x)+M(x)=B(x)
.
Finally,
\mathsf{Adv}
does not learn anything new about
s
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, since the verification-points and the signing-polynomial are sent only to
\mathsf{R}
, who is
honest

as per the lemma conditions.

We next prove the unforgeability property, for which we have to consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{I}
.

Lemma 41

If
\mathsf{S},\mathsf{R}
are honest,
\mathsf{I}
is corrupt
and if
\mathsf{R}
outputs
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, then
s^{\prime}=s
holds except with
probability at most
\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\frac{nt}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
, where
t=\max\{|Z|:Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}\}
, irrespective of the network type.

Proof

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of
honest
parties in
\mathcal{P}
and let
Z^{\star}=\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{H}
be the set of
corrupt
parties.
Since
\mathsf{R}
outputs
s^{\prime}
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, it implies that during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, the variable

\mathsf{authCompleted}_{(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R})}
is set to
1
by
\mathsf{R}
. This further implies that

\mathsf{S}
has broadcasted an
\mathsf{OK}
message during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, which also
implies that during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
,

\mathsf{I}
had broadcasted a
t
-degree blinded-polynomial
B(x)
, and
\mathsf{S}
broadcasted the set
\mathcal{SV}
. Furthermore,

\mathsf{S}
has verified that

B(\alpha_{i})=dv_{i}+m_{i}
holds for every verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{SV}
.
Now during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, if
\mathsf{I}
sends
F(x)
as
\mathsf{ICSig}(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R},s)
to
\mathsf{R}
, then

s^{\prime}=s
holds with probability
1
.
So, consider the case when
\mathsf{I}
sends
F^{\prime}(x)
as
\mathsf{ICSig}(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R},s)
to
\mathsf{R}
, where

F^{\prime}(x)
is a
t
-degree polynomial such that
F^{\prime}(x)\neq F(x)
and where
F^{\prime}(0)=s^{\prime}
. In this case, we claim that except with probability at most
\frac{nt}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
,
the verification-point of no
honest
verifier from
\mathcal{SV}
will get accepted by
\mathsf{R}
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, with respect to
F^{\prime}(x)
. Now, assuming that the claim is true, the
proof follows using the following arguments, depending upon the network type.

–

Synchronous Network
:
In this case, all the verifiers in
\mathcal{H}
will be present in
\mathcal{SV}
. This is because, each verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
would have received its verification-point from
\mathsf{S}
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, within time
\Delta
and
indicates this by broadcasting
(\mathsf{Received},i)
, which is received by
\mathsf{S}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. Let
\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}
be the set of verifiers from which
\mathsf{R}
receives verification points which it accepts. Since the
verification point of
none
of the honest verifier will be accepted.
Hence
(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV}^{\prime})=\emptyset
and so

\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}
must hold. Since
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{1}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
will
never
hold true. Hence,
\mathsf{R}
will
not
output
s^{\prime}\neq s
.

–

Asynchronous Network
: In this case, we first note that
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1,1)}(\mathcal{SV},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition. This is because

\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{SV}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition. From the steps of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, it follow that for

\mathsf{R}
to output
F^{\prime}(0)
,
\mathsf{R}
should find a subset of verifiers
\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{SV}
, where
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that the verification-points of all the verifiers in

\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}
are accepted by
\mathsf{R}
. This further implies that
\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{H}\neq\emptyset
, as
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{SV}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition.
And hence
\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}
has at least one
honest
verifier, whose verification-point is accepted with respect to
F^{\prime}(x)
. However, from the above claim, it is not possible and hence

\mathsf{R}
will
not
output
s^{\prime}\neq s
.

We now prove the claimed statement. So consider an
arbitrary
verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV}
from whom
\mathsf{R}
receives the verification-point
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.
This point can be
accepted
with respect to
F^{\prime}(x)
, only if either of the following holds.

\bullet

v_{i}=F^{\prime}(\alpha_{i})
:
This is possible with probability at most
\frac{t}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
.
This is because
F^{\prime}(x)
and
F(x)
, being distinct
t
-degree polynomials, can have at most
t
points in common.
And the evaluation-point
\alpha_{i}
corresponding to
P_{i}
, being randomly selected from
\mathbb{F}-\{0\}
, will
not
be known to

\mathsf{I}
.

\bullet

dv_{i}+m_{i}\neq B(\alpha_{i})
: This is impossible, as otherwise
\mathsf{S}
would have
not
broadcasted
\mathsf{OK}
during

\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, which is a contradiction.

As there could be up to
n-1

honest
verifiers in
\mathcal{SV}
, it follows from the union bound that except with probability at most
\frac{nt}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
,
the verification-point of no
honest
verifier from
\mathcal{SV}
will get accepted by
\mathsf{R}
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, with respect to
F^{\prime}(x)
.

We next prove the non-repudiation property, for which we have to consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{S}
.

Lemma 42

If
\mathsf{S}
is corrupt,
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R}
are honest
and if
\mathsf{I}
sets
\mathsf{ICSig}(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R},s)
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, then the following hold, except with probability at most
\frac{n}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Non-Repudiation
: In a synchronous network,
\mathsf{R}
outputs
s
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, which takes
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}=\Delta
time.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Non-Repudiation
: In an asynchronous network,
\mathsf{R}
eventually outputs
s
during during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.

Proof

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of honest parties in
\mathcal{P}
and
Z^{\star}
be the set of corrupt parties, where
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
.
Since
\mathsf{I}
has set
\mathsf{ICSig}(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R},s)
during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, it implies that
\mathsf{I}
has set the variable

\mathsf{authCompleted}_{(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R})}
to
1
. This further implies that
\mathsf{I}
has broadcasted

(d,B(x))
, where
B(x)=dF(x)+M(x)
, and where
F(x)
and
M(x)
are the
t
-degree
signing and masking-polynomials received by
\mathsf{I}
from
\mathsf{S}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{I}
also received the set of supporting verifiers
\mathcal{SV}
from the broadcast of
\mathsf{S}
, and verified that
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{SV}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
holds.
Furthermore,
\mathsf{S}
has broadcasted an
\mathsf{OK}
message.
Consequently, from the
consistency
properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
(see Theorem
3.2
),
irrespective of the network type, all
honest
parties including
\mathsf{R}
eventually set
\mathsf{authCompleted}_{(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R})}
to
1
.
Moreover,

\mathsf{I}
sets
\mathsf{ICSig}(\mathsf{S},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{R},s)
to
F(x)
, where

s=F(0)
.
During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
,
\mathsf{I}
sends

F(x)
to
\mathsf{R}
. Moreover, every verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV}
sends its verification-point

(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
to
\mathsf{R}
.
In a
synchronous
network, these will be received by
\mathsf{R}
within time
\Delta
,
while in an asynchronous network, these will be eventually received by
\mathsf{R}
.
We claim that except with probability at most
\frac{n}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
,
all these verification-points are accepted by
\mathsf{R}
.
Now, assuming that the claim is true, the proof follows from the fact that

\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV}=\mathcal{SV}\setminus Z^{\star}
, and
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
holds,
irrespective
of the network type (since
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
).
Consequently,
\mathsf{R}
accepts the verification-points from a subset of the verifiers

\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{SV}
where
\mathcal{SV}\setminus\mathcal{SV}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. And hence it outputs
s
, either within
time
\Delta
in a synchronous network, or eventually, in an asynchronous network.

We now proceed to prove the claim. So consider an
arbitrary
verifier
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{SV}
whose verification-point
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
is received by
\mathsf{R}

during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. Now, there are two possible cases, depending upon the relationship that holds between
F(\alpha_{i})
and
v_{i}

during
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
.

–

v_{i}=F(\alpha_{i})
holds
: In this case, according to the protocol steps of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, the point
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
is
accepted
by
\mathsf{R}
.

–

v_{i}\neq F(\alpha_{i})
holds
: In this case, we claim that except with probability at most
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
, the condition

dv_{i}+m_{i}\neq B(\alpha_{i})
will hold, implying that the point
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
is
accepted
by
\mathsf{R}
.
This is because the
only
way
dv_{i}+m_{i}=B(\alpha_{i})
holds is when
\mathsf{S}
distributes
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
to
P_{i}

where
v_{i}\neq F(\alpha_{i})
and
m_{i}\neq M(\alpha_{i})
holds, and
\mathsf{I}
selects
d=(M(\alpha_{i})-m_{i})\cdot(v_{i}-F(\alpha_{i}))^{-1}
.
However,
\mathsf{S}
will
not
be knowing the random
d
from
\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\}
which
\mathsf{I}
is going to pick, while distributing

F(x),M(x)
to
\mathsf{I}
, and
(\alpha_{i},v_{i},m_{i})
to
P_{i}
. Hence, the probability that
\mathsf{I}
indeed selects
d=(M(\alpha_{i})-m_{i})\cdot(v_{i}-F(\alpha_{i}))^{-1}

is
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
.

As there can be up to
n-1

honest
verifiers in
\mathcal{SV}
, from the union bound, it follows that except with probability at most

\frac{n}{|\mathbb{F}|-1}
, the verification-point of
all
honest verifiers in
\mathcal{SV}
are accepted by
\mathsf{R}
.

We finally derive the communication complexity.

Lemma 43

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n^{5}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits. Protocol

\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|})
bits.

Proof

During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, signer
\mathsf{S}
sends
t
-degree polynomials

F(x)
and
M(x)
to
\mathsf{I}
, and verification-points to each verifier. This requires a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|})
bits.
Intermediary
\mathsf{I}
needs to broadcast
B(x)
and
d
using protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
while
\mathsf{S}
needs to broadcast
the set
\mathcal{SV}
using
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{S}
may need to broadcast
s
using
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
By substituting the communication cost of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
(see Theorem
3.2
), the overall communication cost of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
turns out to be

\mathcal{O}(n^{5}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits.
During
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
,
\mathsf{I}
may send
F(x)
to
\mathsf{R}
, and each verifier may send its verification-point to
\mathsf{R}
.
This incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|})
bits.

The proof of Theorem
4.1
now follows easily from Lemma
39
-
43
.

Appendix 0.C
Properties of Network Agnostic VSS

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
(see Fig
7
). Throughout this section, we assume that
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the conditions

\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
,
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
,
\mathbb{Q}^{(3)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
and
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
.
We start with the properties in a
synchronous
network and first consider an
honest

\mathsf{D}
. We first show that an
honest

\mathsf{D}
will broadcast some candidate core-sets, which will be accepted by all honest parties. Moreover, adversary will not learn
any additional information about
s
.

Lemma 44

If the network is synchronous and
\mathsf{D}
is honest,
participating in
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
with input
s
, then all the following hold, where
\mathcal{H}
is the set of honest parties.

–

There exists some
S_{p}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, such that
\mathsf{D}
broadcasts a message

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
and every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
includes
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to the set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Moreover, all the following hold for
q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
.

–

If
S_{q}=\mathcal{H}
, then
q\not\in\mathcal{BS}
.

–

\mathcal{W}_{q}
will be either
S_{q}
or
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
. Moreover,

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
will satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition.

–

Corresponding to every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
will have the IC-signature

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
of every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
for every
P_{k}\not\in S_{q}
, such that
the underlying signatures will satisfy the linearity principle.
Furthermore, if any corrupt
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
has the IC-signature
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s^{\prime}_{q})
of any honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
for any
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, then

s^{\prime}_{q}=s_{q}
holds and the underlying signatures will satisfy the linearity principle.

–

Corresponding to every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
,
every honest
P_{i}\in S_{q}
will have the share
s_{q}
, except with a probability
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}+T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, where

s=s_{1}+\ldots+s_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
.

–

The view of the adversary will be independent of
s
.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
We note that
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
.
Since
\mathsf{D}
is honest, it picks the shares
s_{1},\dots,s_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
such that
s=s_{1}+\dots+s_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
and sends
s_{q}
to each party
P_{i}\in S_{q}
, corresponding to
every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
. These shares are delivered within time
\Delta
. Now consider an
arbitrary

S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
.
At time
\Delta
, each party
P_{i}\in(S_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
starts giving

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s_{qi})
to every
P_{j}\in S_{q}
, for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, where
s_{qi}=s_{q}
holds.
Moreover, the linearity principle is followed while generating these IC-signatures.
Then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
4.1
),
it follows that at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, each party
P_{i}\in(S_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
will receive

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{qj})
from every
P_{j}\in(S_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
, for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, such that
s_{qj}=s_{qi}=s_{q}
holds.
Since
S_{q}
is arbitrary, it follows that at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}
, every party
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
broadcasts an
\mathsf{OK}(i,j)
message, corresponding to every

P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
), it follows that
these
\mathsf{OK}(i,j)
messages are received by every party in
\mathcal{H}
through
regular-mode
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Consequently, the set
\mathcal{H}
constitutes a clique in the consistency graph of every party in
\mathcal{H}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Now since the set
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, it follows that at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, there exists
some

S_{p}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, such that
\mathsf{D}
computes the core-sets
\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
and broadcast-set
\mathcal{BS}
, followed by
broadcasting
(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Moreover, since
\mathsf{D}
is
honest
, it computes the sets
\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
and
\mathcal{BS}
honestly, satisfying the following conditions, for

q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
.

–

If
S_{q}
constitutes a clique in the graph
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
, then
\mathcal{W}_{q}=S_{q}
.

–

Else if
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
constitutes a clique in
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
and

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{p}\cap S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
then
\mathcal{W}_{q}=(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
.

–

Else
\mathcal{W}_{q}=S_{q}
and
q\in\mathcal{BS}
.

Note that for each
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds. This is obviously true if
\mathcal{W}_{q}=(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
, since in this case
\mathsf{D}
also checks that

\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{p}\cap S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition holds. On the other hand, even if
\mathcal{W}_{q}=S_{q}
, the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds, as

\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds due to the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition.
We also note that if
S_{q}=\mathcal{H}
, then
q\notin\mathcal{BS}
and consequently,
\mathsf{D}
does not make public the share
s_{q}
. This is because
as shown above, at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, the parties in
S_{q}
constitute a clique in the graph
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
.

Since
\mathsf{D}
broadcasts
(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network, it follows that all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will receive

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
from the broadcast of

\mathsf{D}
, at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. To show that every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will include
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to the set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, we need to show that all the conditions which hold
for
\mathsf{D}
in its graph
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, are bound to hold for every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, at the time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
Namely, all the edges which are present in
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, are bound to be present in the
graph
G^{(i)}
every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, at the time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
However, this simply follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity,
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
)
and the fact that edges are added to consistency graphs, based on the receipt of
\mathsf{OK}(\star,\star)
messages, which are broadcasted through various
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
instances.
Consequently, any edge
(i,j)
which is included in
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
at the time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, is bound to be included in the graph
G^{(i)}
of every

P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, latest by time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.

We next note that corresponding to every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
will have the share
s_{q}
, which is either made public by
\mathsf{D}

as part of the
\mathsf{CanCS}
message
or received from
\mathsf{D}
. Each honest
P_{i}\in W_{q}
will thus set
[s]_{q}
to
s_{q}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.
We next show that each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will have
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
corresponding to
every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, for every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
and every
P_{k}\not\in S_{q}
.
This also is set at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. This is because there are two possible cases with respect to
q
.
If
q\in\mathcal{BS}
, then from the protocol steps,
\mathcal{W}_{q}
is publicly set to
S_{q}
and
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
is set to the default value.
On the other hand, if
q\not\in\mathcal{BS}
, then also
P_{i}
will possess
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
. This is because as per the protocol steps,
since
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, it follows that

P_{i}
must have verified that the edge
(P_{i},P_{j})\in G^{(i)}
, which further implies that
P_{i}
has received
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{qj})
from
P_{j}
, where
s_{qj}=s_{qi}
holds.
And since
\mathsf{D}
is
honest
,
s_{qi}=s_{q}
holds, implying that
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{qj})
is the same as
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
.
On the other hand, in the protocol,
P_{i}
gives
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s_{qi})
to every
P_{j}\in S_{q}
for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, where
s_{qi}=s_{q}
holds.
Hence if any corrupt
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
has
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s^{\prime}_{q})
of any honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}

for any
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, then

s^{\prime}_{q}=s_{q}
holds.

We now show that, corresponding to each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
sets
[s]_{q}
to
s_{q}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}+T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. For this, we first show that
P_{i}
sets
[s]_{q}
to
some
value.
Since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition, the set

\mathcal{W}_{q}
contains
at least
one
honest
party, say
P_{j}
. Since
P_{j}
follows the protocol steps honestly, it reveals

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s]_{q})
of every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
to
P_{i}
, at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of ICP in the
synchronous
network
(see Theorem
4.1
), it follows that
P_{i}
will accept these signatures
after time
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. On the other hand, even if
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
is
corrupt
, then also from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-non-repudiation
property of ICP in the
synchronous
network
(see Theorem
4.1
), it follows that
P_{i}
accepts
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s]_{q})
, except with a probability
\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}}
, after time
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. As there can be
\mathcal{O}(n)

corrupt
parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, from the union bound, it follows that
except with a probability
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, party
P_{i}
will find a candidate party from
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, who reveals
[s]_{q}
, along with the IC-signature of all the parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, after time
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. Now as there can be
\mathcal{O}(n)
parties in
S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}
, it follows that except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, every honest party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}
will find a candidate party from
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, who reveals
[s]_{q}
along with the IC-signature of all the parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}+T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.

We next show that
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
indeed sets
[s]_{q}
to
s_{q}
. Suppose that
P_{i}
sets
[s]_{q}
to some value
s^{\prime}
. From the protocols steps, this implies that there exists some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, such that
P_{i}
has accepted

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s^{\prime})
of every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, revealed by
P_{j}
. If
P_{j}
is
honest
, then indeed
s^{\prime}=[s]_{q}
, as one of the IC-signatures

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s^{\prime})
is the same as
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s]_{q})
, corresponding to the
honest

P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, which is guaranteed to exist.
So consider the case when
P_{j}
is
corrupt
.
Moreover, let
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
be an
honest
party (which is guaranteed to exist).
In order that
s^{\prime}\neq[s]_{q}
, it must be the case that
P_{i}
accepts
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s^{\prime})
, revealed by
P_{j}
. However, from
the
unforgeability
property of ICP (see Theorem
4.1
), this can happen only with probability
\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}}
. Now as there can be up to
\mathcal{O}(n)
corrupt parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, from the union bound, it follows that the probability that
P_{i}
outputs
s^{\prime}\neq[s]_{q}
is at most
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. Since there can be up to
\mathcal{O}(n)
parties in
S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}
, it follows that except with probability at most

\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the output of every honest party in
S_{q}
is indeed
s_{q}
. Now, there can be
|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|
possibilities for
S_{q}
. From the union bound, it follows that corresponding to each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
sets
[s]_{q}
to
s_{q}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

Finally, the privacy for
s
follows from the fact that throughout the protocol, the view of the adversary remains independent of the share
s_{q}
, corresponding to the group
S_{q}
, where

S_{q}=\mathcal{H}
. This is because as shown above,
q\notin\mathcal{BS}
and consequently,
\mathsf{D}
does not make public the share
s_{q}
.
Moreover, during the pairwise consistency tests, the view of the adversary remains independent of
s_{q}
, when the parties in
\mathcal{H}
exchange IC-signed
s_{q}
, which
follows from the
privacy
property of ICP (see Theorem
4.1
). Further, while computing the output,
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s_{q})
is revealed by party
P_{j}\in W_{q}
only to each party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
. Hence, the adversary does not learn
s_{q}
.

An immediate corollary of Lemma
44
is that if
\mathsf{D}
is honest, then the parties output
[s]
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}+T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, which follows
from the definition of
[\cdot]
-sharing.

Corollary 2

If the network is synchronous and
\mathsf{D}
is honest and
participates in
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
with input
s
, then the parties output
[s]
at time
\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}+T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

We next consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{D}
in the
synchronous
network and show that if any honest party computes an output at time
T
, then there exists some
s^{\star}\in\mathbb{F}
such that the (honest)
parties output
[s^{\star}]
by time
T+\Delta
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

Lemma 45

If the network is synchronous and
\mathsf{D}
is corrupt and if any honest party computes an output at time
T
, then there exists some
s^{\star}\in\mathbb{F}
, such that the honest parties output

[s^{\star}]
by time
T+\Delta
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties. Let
P_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}
be the
first
honest party
which computes an output at time
T
. The way in which the core sets are defined ensures that each set in
\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
must contain at least one honest party. Since
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
takes at least
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
time to complete, this means that there exists
S_{p}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
such that some honest party
P_{m}
receives a message

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
from the broadcast
of
\mathsf{D}
by the time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
and includes
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to
\mathcal{C}_{m}
.
This further implies that
P_{m}
has verified that the following hold, for

q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
, by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.

–

If
q\not\in\mathcal{BS}
, then
\mathcal{W}_{q}
is either
S_{q}
or
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
. Moreover,

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition
and the parties in

\mathcal{W}_{q}
constitute a clique in the graph
G^{(m)}
.

–

If
q\in\mathcal{BS}
, then
\mathsf{D}
has made public
s_{q}
, as part of the
\mathsf{CanCS}
message. Moreover,
\mathcal{W}_{q}
is set to
S_{q}
.

From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
), it follows that every party in
\mathcal{H}
will receive

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
from the broadcast
of
\mathsf{D}
, latest by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
. We next show that each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will include
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, latest by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
.
For this, it is enough to show that all the edges which are present in
G^{(m)}
at time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
, are bound to be present in the
graph
G^{(i)}
every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, by the time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
.
However, this simply follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity,
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
)
and the fact that edges are added to consistency graphs, based on the receipt of
\mathsf{OK}(\star,\star)
messages, which are broadcasted through various
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
instances.
Consequently, any edge
(i,j)
which is included in
G^{(m)}
at the time
T
, is bound to be included in the graph
G^{(i)}
of every

P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
, latest by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
.

We next show that by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
, corresponding to every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
will have a common share, say
s^{\star}_{q}
.
If
q\in\mathcal{BS}
, this is trivially true, since in this case,
\mathsf{D}
makes public the share
s_{q}
and hence
s^{\star}_{q}=s_{q}
.
On the other hand, consider the case when
q\not\in\mathcal{BS}
and consider
arbitrary

P_{i},P_{j}\in(\mathcal{W}_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
. Since
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
are part of a clique,
it follows that
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
have broadcasted the messages
\mathsf{OK}(i,j)
and
\mathsf{OK}(j,i)
respectively. Moreover, these messages were broadcasted, latest by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}-T_{\mathsf{BC}}
, since
it takes at least
T_{\mathsf{BC}}
time to compute any output in an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
).
Now since
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
have broadcasted
\mathsf{OK}(i,j)
and
\mathsf{OK}(j,i)
messages, it implies that they have verified that
s_{qi}=s_{qj}
holds, where

s_{qi}
and
s_{qj}
are the shares, received by
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
respectively, from
\mathsf{D}
. Let
s_{qi}=s_{qj}=s^{\star}_{q}
.
We define

s^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\displaystyle\sum_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}s^{\star}_{q}.

Till now we have shown that there exists some
s^{\star}\in\mathbb{F}
, such that by time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have the core-sets
\mathcal{W}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{W}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
, where

S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
, for
q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
and where each
P_{i}\in(\mathcal{W}_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
will have a common share
[s^{\star}]_{q}
.
We need to show that by the time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have the required IC-signatures, as part of
[s^{\star}]
, satisfying the linearity property.
Namely, each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will have
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},[s^{\star}]_{q})
, corresponding to
every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, of every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
and for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
. There are two possible cases with respect to
q
.
If
q\in\mathcal{BS}
, then from the protocol steps,
\mathcal{W}_{q}
is publicly set to
S_{q}
and
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},[s^{\star}]_{q})
is set to the default value of

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
, where
s_{q}
is made public by
\mathsf{D}
. As per our notations,
s_{q}=s^{\star}_{q}
for every

q\in\mathcal{BS}
.
On the other hand, if
q\not\in\mathcal{BS}
, then also
P_{i}
will possess
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},[s^{\star}]_{q})
. This is because as per the protocol steps,
since
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, it follows that

P_{i}
must have verified that the edge
(P_{i},P_{j})\in G^{(i)}
, which further implies that
P_{i}
has received
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{qj})
from
P_{j}
, where
s_{qj}=s_{qi}
holds.
Here
s_{qi}
is the share received by
P_{i}
from
\mathsf{D}
and as per our notation,
s_{qi}=s^{\star}_{q}
.
Hence
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{qj})
is the same as
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},[s^{\star}]_{q})
.
On the other hand, in the protocol,
P_{i}
gives
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s_{qi})
to every
P_{j}\in S_{q}
for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, where
s_{qi}=[s^{\star}]_{q}
holds.
Hence if any corrupt
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
has
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s^{\prime}_{q})
, of any honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}

for any
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, then

s^{\prime}_{q}=[s^{\star}]_{q}
holds. It is easy to see that all the underlying IC-signatures will be linear since the parties follow the linearity principle while generating IC-signatures.

Finally, we now show that every honest party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
gets
[s^{\star}]_{q}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at time
T+\Delta
. For this, we first show that
P_{i}
computes some share on the behalf of
S_{q}
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition, the set

\mathcal{W}_{q}
contains
at least
one
honest
party, say
P_{j}
. Since
P_{j}
follows the protocol steps honestly, it reveals
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s^{\star}]_{q})
of every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
to
P_{i}
,
at time
T-T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}+\Delta
. From
the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of ICP in the
synchronous
network
(see Theorem
4.1
), it follows that
P_{i}
will accept the IC-signatures
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s^{\star}]_{q})
, revealed by
P_{j}
,
after time
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. On the other hand, even if
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
is
corrupt
, then also from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-non-repudiation
property of ICP in the
synchronous
network (see Theorem
4.1
), it follows that
P_{i}
accepts
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s^{\star}]_{q})
, except with a probability
\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}}
, after time
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. As there can be
\mathcal{O}(n)

corrupt
parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, from the union bound, it follows that
except with a probability
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, party
P_{i}
will find a candidate party from
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, who reveals
[s^{\star}]_{q}
, along with the IC-signature of all the parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, after time
T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
. Now as there can be
\mathcal{O}(n)
parties in
S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}
, it follows that except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, every honest party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}
will find a candidate party from
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, who reveals
[s^{\star}]_{q}
along with the IC-signature of all the parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
at time
T+\Delta
.

We next show that
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
indeed sets
[s^{\star}]_{q}
as the share corresponding to
S_{q}
.
Suppose that
P_{i}
sets the share to some value
s^{\prime}
. From the protocol steps, this implies that there exists some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, such that
P_{i}
has accepted
the IC-signatures
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s^{\prime})
of every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, revealed by
P_{j}
. If
P_{j}
is
honest
, then indeed
s^{\prime}=[s^{\star}]_{q}
, as one of the IC-signatures

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s^{\prime})
is the same as
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s^{\star}]_{q})
, corresponding to the
honest

P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, which is guaranteed to exist.
So consider the case when
P_{j}
is
corrupt
.
Moreover, let
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
be an
honest
party (which is guaranteed to exist).
In order that
s^{\prime}\neq[s^{\star}]_{q}
, it must be the case that
P_{i}
accepts
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s^{\prime})
, revealed by
P_{j}
. However, from
the
unforgeability
property of ICP (see Theorem
4.1
), this can happen only with probability
\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}}
. Now as there can be up to
\mathcal{O}(n)
corrupt parties in
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, from the union bound, it follows that the probability that
P_{i}
outputs
s^{\prime}\neq[s^{\star}]_{q}
is at most
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. Since there can be up to
\mathcal{O}(n)
parties in
S_{q}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{q}
, it follows that except with probability at most

\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the output of every honest party in
S_{q}
is indeed
[s^{\star}]_{q}
. Now, there can be
|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|
possibilities for
S_{q}
. From the union bound, it follows that corresponding to each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest party in
S_{q}
outputs
[s^{\star}]_{q}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

We next consider an
asynchronous
network and prove the analogue of Lemma
44
by showing that if
\mathsf{D}
is
honest
, then the parties eventually output
[s]

except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. Further, the adversary learns no additional information about
s
. The proof of the lemma follows closely the proof of Lemma
44
,
except that we now rely on the properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
, and ICP in the
asynchronous
network.

Lemma 46

If the network is asynchronous and
\mathsf{D}
is honest,
participating in
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
with input
s
, then the honest parties eventually output
[s]
except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, with the view of the adversary remaining independent of
s
.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
We note that
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, since
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
as
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Since
\mathsf{D}
is honest, it picks the shares
s_{1},\dots,s_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
such that
s=s_{1}+\dots+s_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
and sends
s_{q}
to each party
P_{i}\in S_{q}
, corresponding to
every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
. These shares are eventually delivered. Now consider an
arbitrary

S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
.
After receiving the share
s_{qi}
from
\mathsf{D}
, each party
P_{i}\in(S_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
starts giving

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s_{qi})
to every
P_{j}\in S_{q}
, for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, where
s_{qi}=s_{q}
holds,
such that the linearity principle is followed while generating these IC-signatures.
Then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
4.1
),
it follows that each party
P_{i}\in(S_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
will eventually receive

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{qj})
from every
P_{j}\in(S_{q}\cap\mathcal{H})
, for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, such that
s_{qj}=s_{qi}=s_{q}
holds.
Since
S_{q}
is arbitrary, it follows that eventually, every party
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
broadcasts an
\mathsf{OK}(i,j)
message, corresponding to every

P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
), it follows that
these
\mathsf{OK}(i,j)
messages are eventually received by every party in
\mathcal{H}
.
Consequently, the set
\mathcal{H}
eventually constitutes a clique in the consistency graph of every party in
\mathcal{H}
.
Now since the set
\mathcal{H}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, it follows that eventually, there exists
some

S_{p}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, such that
\mathsf{D}
computes the core-sets
\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
and broadcast-set
\mathcal{BS}
, followed by
broadcasting
(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
.
Moreover, since
\mathsf{D}
is
honest
, it computes the sets
\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
and
\mathcal{BS}
honestly, satisfying the following conditions, for

q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
.

–

If
S_{q}
constitutes a clique in the graph
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
, then
\mathcal{W}_{q}=S_{q}
.

–

Else if
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
constitutes a clique in
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
and

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{p}\cap S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
then
\mathcal{W}_{q}=(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
.

–

Else
\mathcal{W}_{q}=S_{q}
and
q\in\mathcal{BS}
.

Note that for each
\mathcal{W}_{q}
, the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds. This is obviously true if
\mathcal{W}_{q}=(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
, since in this case
\mathsf{D}
also checks that

\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{p}\cap S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds. On the other hand, even if
\mathcal{W}_{q}=S_{q}
,
the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds, since
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
holds.
We also note that if
S_{q}=\mathcal{H}
, then
q\notin\mathcal{BS}
and consequently,
\mathsf{D}
does not make public the share
s_{q}
. This is because
the parties in
S_{p}\cap S_{q}
will constitute a clique and the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(S_{p}\cap S_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition will be satisfied due to the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})

condition. Hence
\mathcal{W}_{q}
will be set to
S_{p}\cap S_{q}
.

Since
\mathsf{D}
eventually broadcasts
(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
,
from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network, it follows that all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually receive

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
from the broadcast of

\mathsf{D}
. We next show that every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will eventually include
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to the set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. For this, we need to show that all the conditions which hold
for
\mathsf{D}
in its graph
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
when it broadcasts the
\mathsf{CanCS}
message,
are bound to eventually hold for every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
.
However, this simply follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity,
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback validity,
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback consistency
of

\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
)
and the fact that edges are added to consistency graphs, based on the receipt of
\mathsf{OK}(\star,\star)
messages, which are broadcasted through various
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
instances.
Consequently, any edge
(i,j)
which is included in
G^{(\mathsf{D})}
, is bound to be eventually included in the graph
G^{(i)}
of every

P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
.

Note that corresponding to every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
will have the share
s_{q}
, which is either made public by
\mathsf{D}

as part of the
\mathsf{CanCS}
message
or received from
\mathsf{D}
.
Next, we note that each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will have
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{j},P_{i},P_{k},s_{q})
, corresponding to
every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
where
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
, from every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
and for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
.
On the other hand, if any corrupt
P_{j}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
has
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{i},P_{j},P_{k},s^{\prime}_{q})
of any honest
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
for
any
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
, then

s^{\prime}_{q}=s_{q}
holds.
The proof for this is exactly the same as that of Lemma
44
.

We now show that every honest party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
eventually sets
[s]_{q}
to
s_{q}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. We first show that
P_{i}
sets
[s]_{q}
to
some
value. Since

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition,
this means that
\mathcal{W}_{q}
contains
at least
one
honest
party, say
P_{j}
. Since
P_{j}
follows the protocol steps honestly, it reveals

\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s]_{q})
of every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
to
P_{i}
. From the

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
property of ICP in the
asynchronous
network
(see Theorem
4.1
), it follows that
P_{i}
will eventually accept these signatures.
On the other hand, even if

P_{k}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
is
corrupt
, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-non-repudiation
property of ICP in the
asynchronous
network
(see Theorem
4.1
), it follows that
P_{i}
eventually accepts
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},[s]_{q})
, except with a probability
\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}}
. From the union bound, it follows that except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, each party
P_{i}\in S_{q}
outputs some value for
[s]_{q}
. Further, this value must be
s_{q}
. The proof of this follows from what was shown in
44
. Now, there can be
|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|
possibilities for
S_{q}
. From the union bound, it follows that corresponding to each
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every honest party in
S_{q}
eventually outputs
[s]_{q}
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

Finally, the privacy for
s
follows from the fact that throughout the protocol, the view of the adversary remains independent of the share
s_{q}
, corresponding to the group
S_{q}
, where

S_{q}=\mathcal{H}
. This is because as shown above,
q\notin\mathcal{BS}
and consequently,
\mathsf{D}
does not make public the share
s_{q}
.
Moreover, during the pairwise consistency tests, the view of the adversary remains independent of
s_{q}
, when the parties in
\mathcal{H}
exchange IC-signed
s_{q}
, which
follows from the
privacy
property of ICP (see Theorem
4.1
). Further, while computing the output,
\mathsf{ICSig}(P_{k},P_{j},P_{i},s_{q})
is revealed by party
P_{j}\in W_{q}
only to each party
P_{i}\in S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
. Hence, the adversary does not learn
s_{q}
.

Finally, we consider a
corrupt

\mathsf{D}
in the
asynchronous
network and prove the analogue of Lemma
45
, whose proof is very similar to that
of Lemma
45
.

Lemma 47

If the network is asynchronous and
\mathsf{D}
is corrupt and if any honest party computes an output, then there exists some
s^{\star}\in\mathbb{F}
, such that the honest parties eventually output

[s^{\star}]
, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties. Let
P_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be the
first
honest party,
who computes an output in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
. This implies that there exists some
S_{p}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, such that
P_{m}
receives a message

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
from the broadcast
of
\mathsf{D}
, and includes
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to
\mathcal{C}_{m}
.
This further implies that
P_{m}
has verified that the following hold, for

q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
.

–

If
q\not\in\mathcal{BS}
, then
\mathcal{W}_{q}
is either
S_{q}
or
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
. Moreover,

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{W}_{q},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition
and the parties in

\mathcal{W}_{q}
constitute a clique in the graph
G^{(m)}
.

–

If
q\in\mathcal{BS}
, then
\mathsf{D}
has made public
s_{q}
, as part of the
\mathsf{CanCS}
message. Moreover,
\mathcal{W}_{q}
is set to
S_{q}
.

From the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
),
it follows that every party in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually receive

(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
from the broadcast
of
\mathsf{D}
. We next show that each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
will eventually include
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}
.
For this, it is enough to show that all the edges which are present in
G^{(m)}
when
(\mathsf{D},S_{p})
is included in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
are bound to be eventually present in the
graph
G^{(i)}
of every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
.
However, this simply follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak validity,
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback validity,
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the

asynchronous
network (see Theorem
3.2
)
and the fact that edges are added to consistency graphs, based on the receipt of
\mathsf{OK}(\star,\star)
messages, which are broadcasted through various
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
instances.

Next, it can be shown that corresponding to every
S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{q}
will have a common share, say
s^{\star}_{q}
.
The proof for this is the same as Lemma
45
.
We define

s^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\displaystyle\sum_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}s^{\star}_{q}.

Now similar to the proof of Lemma
45
, it can be shown that each party
P_{i}
in
(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{W}_{q})
will eventually have the required IC-signatures
on
[s^{\star}]_{q}
as part of
[s^{\star}]
and
will reveal these to parties in
S_{q}\setminus W_{q}
. Consequently, each party in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually set
[s^{\star}]_{q}
as the share corresponding to
S_{q}

and hence,
s^{\star}
will eventually be secret-shared, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.

We next derive the communication complexity of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
.

Lemma 48

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{8}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits.

Proof

In the protocol,
\mathsf{D}
needs to send the share
s_{q}
to
all
the parties in
S_{q}
. This incurs a total communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|})
bits. There are

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{3})
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
invoked, to exchange IC-signed values, during the pairwise consistency tests. From Theorem
4.1
,
this incurs a total communication of

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{8}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits. There are
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})

\mathsf{OK}
messages which need to be broadcasted, which from Theorem
3.2
,
incurs a total communication of

\mathcal{O}(n^{6}\cdot\log{n}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits, since each
\mathsf{OK}
message encodes the identity of two parties, requiring
\log{n}
bits.
Finally,
\mathsf{D}
needs to broadcast a candidate
(\mathsf{CanCS},\mathsf{D},S_{p},\{\mathcal{W}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS},\{s_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}})
message, where

S_{p},\mathcal{BS}
and each
\mathcal{W}_{q}
can be represented by
\mathcal{O}(n)
bits. And corresponding to the indices in
\mathcal{BS}
, the dealer
\mathsf{D}
may end up broadcasting

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|)
shares. From Theorem
3.2
, this incurs a total communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot(n^{5}\cdot|\sigma|+n^{4}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|))
bits. While computing the output,
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|)
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
are involved, which incur a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{4}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|})
bits.

Theorem
5.1
now follows from Lemma
44
-
48
.

Theorem
5.1
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
achieves the following, except with a probability of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|\cdot n^{2}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, where
\mathsf{D}
has input
s\in\mathbb{F}
for
\Pi_{\mathsf{VSS}}
and where

T_{\mathsf{VSS}}=\Delta+T_{\mathsf{Auth}}+2T_{\mathsf{BC}}+T_{\mathsf{Reveal}}
.

–

If
\mathsf{D}
is honest, then the following hold.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
: In a synchronous network, the honest parties output
[s]
at time
