. This is because if
all
the parties in
\mathcal{H}
participates with input
0
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(1,q_{\mathsf{core}})}
, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the

asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
would have obtained the output
0
from the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(1,q_{\mathsf{core}})}
, which is a contradiction.
Now since
P_{k}
has participated with input
1

in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(1,q_{\mathsf{core}})}
, it follows there exists a subset of parties
{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}
, where

\mathcal{CD}\setminus{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that
(P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}})
is present in the set
\mathcal{C}_{k}
, corresponding to every
P_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}
.
We show that the set
{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}
constitutes the candidate
{\mathcal{}B}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}
. For this, note that for any
P_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}
,
party
P_{k}
includes
(P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}})
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, only after receiving
a message
(\mathsf{CanCS},P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{\mathcal{W}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{s^{(\ell)}_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}})

from the broadcast of
P_{\ell}

and verifying it.
It then follows from Lemma
56
that eventually,

every
party in
\mathcal{H}
would have received

(\mathsf{CanCS},P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{\mathcal{W}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{s^{(\ell)}_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}})
from
each
P_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}
. Hence each party
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
would eventually include
(P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}})
to the set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, corresponding to every
P_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{}A}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},k}
.

We next claim that corresponding to every
P_{\ell}\in\mathsf{CORE}
, every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
eventually receives a message

(\mathsf{CanCS},P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{\mathcal{W}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{s^{(\ell)}_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}})
from
the broadcast of
P_{\ell}
. The proof for this is very similar to the proof of the previous claim and relies on the properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network.
So consider an
arbitrary

P_{\ell}\in\mathsf{CORE}
.
This implies that the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(2,\ell)}
have produced output
1
, which further implies that at least one party from
\mathcal{H}
, say
P_{m}
, have participated with input

1
during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(2,\ell)}
. If not, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(2,\ell)}

would have produced output
0
, which is a contradiction.
Now since
P_{m}
participates with input
1
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(2,\ell)}
, it follows that

P_{m}
must have received a message

(\mathsf{CanCS},P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{\mathcal{W}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{s^{(\ell)}_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}})

from the broadcast of
P_{\ell}
and included
(P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}})
to
\mathcal{C}_{m}
.
It then follows from Lemma
56
that eventually,

every
party
P_{i}
in
\mathcal{H}
would receive

(\mathsf{CanCS},P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{\mathcal{W}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{s^{(\ell)}_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}})
from

P_{\ell}
and includes
(P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}})
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

Till now we have shown that almost-surely, all the
|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|
instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(1,\star)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}

and then all the
|\mathcal{CD}|
instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(2,\star)}
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
will eventually produce some output, for all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually have a common
q_{\mathsf{core}}\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\}
and a common set
\mathsf{CORE}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathsf{CORE}
has at least one
honest
party.
Furthermore, corresponding to every
P_{\ell}\in\mathsf{CORE}
, each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
would eventually received a message

(\mathsf{CanCS},P_{\ell},S_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{\mathcal{W}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}},q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}},\{s^{(\ell)}_{q}\}_{q\in\mathcal{BS}^{(\ell)}_{q_{\mathsf{core}}}})

from the broadcast of
P_{\ell}
. The rest of the proof will be now same as Lemma
52
, except that we
now rely on Lemma
54
and Lemma
55
.
To avoid repetition, we do not produce the formal details here.

We finally derive the communication complexity of the protocol.

Lemma 57

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{MDVSS}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|^{2}\cdot n^{9}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits. In addition,

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|+n)
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
are invoked.

Proof

The number of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances follows easily from the protocol inspection. The communication complexity of the protocol is dominated by the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{SVM}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
invoked in the protocol.
There are
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n)
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{SVM}}
and
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{4})
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
invoked. The communication complexity now follows from the communication complexity of

\Pi_{\mathsf{SVM}}
(Lemma
5
) and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Auth}}
(Theorem
4.1
).

Theorem
7.1
now follows from Lemma
52
, Lemma
56
and Lemma
57
.

Appendix 0.F
Properties of the Triple-Generation Protocol

In this section, we prove the properties of the triple-generation protocol and related sub-protocols.

0.F.1
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}

We first prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
(see Fig
10
for the formal description).

Lemma
6
.

Let
r
be a random value which is linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
Then protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
achieves the following where
\mathsf{D}
participates with the input
s
.

–

If
\mathsf{D}
is honest, then the following hold, where
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}=T_{\mathsf{Rec}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Correctness
: If the network is synchronous, then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the honest parties output
[s]
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Correctness
: If the network is asynchronous, then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the honest parties eventually
output
[s]
, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

–

Privacy
: Irrespective of the network type, the view of the adversary remains independent of
s
.

–

If
\mathsf{D}
is corrupt then either no honest party computes any output or there exists some value, say
s^{\star}
, such that the following hold.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-Commitment
: If the network is synchronous, then
except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the honest parties output
[s^{\star}]
, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Moreover, if any honest party computes its output at the time
T
, then all honest parties will have their respective output by
the time
T+\Delta
.

–

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-Commitment
: If the network is asynchronous, then
except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the honest parties eventually output
[s^{\star}]
, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

–

Communication Complexity
:
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{3}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{4}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)
bits are communicated by the honest parties.

Proof

Let us first consider an
honest
dealer. Moreover, we consider a
synchronous
network. Form the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in the
synchronous
network (Lemma
4
),
after time
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
, the dealer
\mathsf{D}
will reconstruct

r
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. Moreover, from the
privacy
property of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
,
r
will be random from the point of the view of the adversary. Since
\mathsf{D}
is
honest
, from the

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
), all honest parties will receive
\mathbf{s}
from the broadcast of
\mathsf{D}
at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}+T_{\mathsf{BC}}
,
where
\mathbf{s}=s+r
. The parties then take the
default linear secret-sharing
[\mathbf{s}]
of
\mathbf{s}
with the IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Since

r
is also linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, from the linearity property of the secret sharing, it follows that

[\mathbf{s}-r]
will be the same as a linear secret-sharing of
s
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
This proves the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
.
The
privacy
of
s
follows since
r
remains random for the adversary and hence
\mathbf{s}

does not
reveal any information about
s
to the adversary.
The
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
and
privacy
for an
honest
dealer in an
asynchronous
network follows using similar arguments as above, except that we now rely on the

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
4
) and the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
).

We next consider a
corrupt
dealer and a
synchronous
network. Let
P_{h}
be the
first
honest party which computes some output in the protocol, at the time
T
. This implies that
P_{h}
has received some value

\mathbf{s}
from the broadcast of
\mathsf{D}
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
), it follows that all the honest parties will receive

\mathbf{s}
from the broadcast of
\mathsf{D}
, latest by the time
T+\Delta
. We define

s^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\mathbf{s}-r.

Since the (honest) parties take the
default linear secret-sharing
[\mathbf{s}]
of
\mathbf{s}
with the IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets
and since
r
is also linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, from the
linearity property of the secret sharing, it follows that

[\mathbf{s}-r]
will be the same as a linear secret-sharing of
s^{\star}
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
This completes the proof of the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-commitment
in the
synchronous
network. The proof of the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-commitment
in the
asynchronous
network is similar as above, except that
we now rely on the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-weak consistency
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-fallback consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.2
)
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
4
).

In the protocol, one instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
with
|\mathcal{R}|=1
and one instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
with
\ell=\log{|\mathbb{F}|}
bits are invoked.
The communication complexity now follows from the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
(Lemma
4
) and communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BC}}
(Theorem
3.2
).

0.F.2
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
(see Fig
11
for the formal details).
While proving these properties, we will assume that
no honest
party is present in the set
\mathcal{GD}
;
looking ahead, this will be ensured in the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
(presented in Section
8.3
), where the set
\mathcal{GD}
is maintained.

We begin by showing that no summand-sharing party during the first two phases are from the discarded set of parties.

Lemma
7
.

During any instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},[a],[b],{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
,
if
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
then
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{GD}
, irrespective of the network type.

Proof

Let
P_{j}
be an
arbitrary
party belonging to the set
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. This implies that
P_{j}
is included to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, either during Phase I or Phase II.
If
P_{j}\in\mathcal{GD}
, then
no honest party will participate with input
1
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
during Phase I for any value of
\mathsf{hop}
and
instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
during Phase II. Consequently, from the

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
(Theorem
3.1
),
party
P_{j}
will not be added to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, which is a contradiction.

We next show that the first phase will get over for the honest parties
after a fixed time in a
synchronous
network and eventually in an asynchronous network. Towards this we show that if the honest parties start any hop during the first phase, then they will complete it after a fixed time
in a
synchronous
network and eventually in an asynchronous network.

Lemma
8
.

Suppose that no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
. If the honest parties start participating during hop number
\mathsf{hop}
of Phase I of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
with iteration number
\mathsf{iter}
,
then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
the hop takes
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
time to complete in a synchronous network, or almost-surely completes eventually in an asynchronous network.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
We note that since
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
,
irrespective
of the network type,
there exists some set in
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
, say
S_{h}
, such that
S_{h}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
.
Since the honest parties participate in hop number
\mathsf{hop}
, it implies that there exists
no

S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
,
such that
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{q})}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\emptyset
.
Particularly, this implies that
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{h})}_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq\emptyset
.
Hence there exists some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, such that
P_{j}\notin\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.
This is because if
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, then clearly
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{h})}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\emptyset
and hence the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will
not
participate in hop number
\mathsf{hop}
.

Let us first consider a
synchronous
network. During the hop number
\mathsf{hop}
,
every

P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
P_{j}\notin\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
will invoke an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
with the input

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. Then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
synchronous
network (Lemma
6
),
after time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
output
[c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
Since
(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{GD})=\emptyset
,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
with input
1
. Hence
from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), all the parties in
\mathcal{H}

output
1
during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
,
after time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
.
Consequently, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
start participating with input
0
in the remaining
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},k)}
(if any), for which no input is provided yet. Hence from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-security
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
synchronous
network, at the
time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
compute some output during the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances invoked during hop number
\mathsf{hop}

and hence complete the hop number
\mathsf{hop}
.

Next, consider an
asynchronous
network.
From the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
6
),
corresponding to
every

P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
P_{j}\notin\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
,
the parties in
\mathcal{H}
eventually output
[c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, during the instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
. Now there are now two cases.

–

Case 1 - There exists some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
where
P_{j}\not\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and some party
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
,
such that

P_{i}
has started participating with input
0
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
: This implies that

P_{i}
has computed the output
1
in some
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instance during the Phase I, say
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},m)}
. And hence
P_{i}
starts participating in all the
remaining

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances of
hop number
\mathsf{hop}
of Phase I
(if any) with the input
0
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will also eventually compute the output
1
during the instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},m)}
and will start participating in all the
remaining

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances of hop number
\mathsf{hop}
of Phase I
(if any) with the input
0
. Consequently, from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-security
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), almost-surely, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
eventually compute some
output during all the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances of hop number
\mathsf{hop}

and hence complete the hop number
\mathsf{hop}
.

–

Case 2 - No honest party has yet started participating with input
0
in any of the BA instances of Phase I corresponding to the honest parties
:
In this case, the honest parties will eventually start participating with input
1
in the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
,
corresponding to the parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
where
P_{j}\not\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. Hence from the

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-validity
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
),
it follows that eventually, there will be some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
where
P_{j}\not\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, such that
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
compute the output
1
during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phI},\mathsf{hop},j)}
. The rest of the proof is similar to what is argued in the previous case.

We next show that in protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, the honest parties compute some output, after a fixed time in a
synchronous
network and eventually in an
asynchronous
network.

Lemma
9
.

If no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
, then in protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
all honest parties compute some output by the time
T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}=(2n+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{BA}}+(n+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network, or almost-surely,
eventually in an asynchronous network.

Proof

In the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, to compute an output, the (honest) parties need to complete the three phases. We first show that

irrespective
of the network type, the honest parties will complete Phase II, provided they complete Phase I.
This is because
the honest parties will participate in
all
the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
of phase II with
some
input,
after waiting
exactly
for time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
. Consequently, once phase I is completed, from the

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-security
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), it takes
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
time
for the honest parties to compute outputs in the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances during phase II in a
synchronous
network.
On the other hand, the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-security
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
)
guarantees that almost-surely, all honest parties
eventually compute some output during the
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances during phase II in an asynchronous network. Now once Phase I and Phase II are completed,
it takes
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
time for the parties to complete Phase III in a
synchronous
network (follows from Lemma
4
),
while in an
asynchronous
network, it gets completed eventually (see Lemma
4
).

We now show that Phase I always gets completed for the honest parties.
Lemma
8
guarantees that if the honest parties start any hop during Phase I, then it gets completed for all the honest parties
after time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
in a
synchronous
network or eventually in an
asynchronous
network.
From the protocol steps, it follows that there can be
at most

n
hops during Phase I. This is because once the set of
honest
parties are included in the set of summand-sharing parties

\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during Phase I, then the parties will exit Phase I.

We next show that the adversary does not learn anything additional about
a
and
b
during the protocol.

Lemma
10
.

If no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
, then the view of the adversary remains independent of
a
and
b
throughout the protocol, irrespective of the network type.

Proof

Let
Z^{\star}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}
be the set of
honest
parties.
The view of the adversary remains independent of
a
and
b
during Phase I and Phase II.
This is because the
privacy
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
(Lemma
6
) ensures that the view of the adversary remains independent of the summand-sums shared by the
honest

summand-sharing parties during Phase I and Phase II. Let
S_{h}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
be the group consisting of
only
honest parties; i.e.
(S_{h}\cap Z^{\star})=\emptyset
.
To prove that the view of the adversary remains independent of
a
and
b
during Phase III, we show that
irrespective
of the network type,
any summand of the form
(h,q)
or
(p,h)

will
not
be present in
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during this phase corresponding to any
p,q\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\}
, implying that the shares
[a]_{h}
and
[b]_{h}

does not
get publicly reconstructed.
Note that
(h,q)\not\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
for any
P_{j}\in Z^{\star}
, as otherwise
it would imply that a
corrupt

P_{j}\in(S_{h}\cap Z^{\star})
, which is a contradiction.
Similarly,
(p,h)\not\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
for any
P_{j}\in Z^{\star}

Let us first consider a
synchronous
network and consider an
arbitrary
ordered pair
(h,q)
.
Consider the case when

(h,q)
has
not been
removed from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during Phase I in any of the hops.
Then, during Phase II, the pair
(h,q)
will get
statically
re-assigned to the
\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
set of some
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, such that
P_{j}\not\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.
From the protocol steps,
P_{j}
will include the summand
[a]_{h}\cdot[b]_{q}
while computing
c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and share

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
through an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
. From the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
synchronous
network, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will output
[c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
during the instance of

\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
. Since
P_{j}\notin(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cup\mathcal{GD})
, it follows that all the parties in
\mathcal{H}

will participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(\mathsf{phII},j)}
with input
1
and compute the output
1
. Consequently,
(h,q)
will be removed from the updated
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during Phase II, if not removed
during Phase I. By the same logic, any ordered pair of the form
(p,h)
will also be removed from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, by the end of Phase II.

Next, consider an
asynchronous
network and an
arbitrary

(h,q)
. In this case, we show
that
(h,q)
will eventually be removed from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during Phase I itself.
Let the parties in
\mathcal{H}
complete Phase I.
This implies that there exists some
S_{\ell}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
such that
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{\ell})}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\emptyset
when Phase I gets over.
We show that
(h,q)
was present in
\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{\ell})}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the beginning of Phase I, when the parties initialize

\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{\ell})}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. That is, at the time of initialization, there was some
P_{j}\in(S_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{H}\cap S_{q})
such that

(h,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. For this, it is enough to show that
(S_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{H}\cap S_{q})\neq\emptyset
, which follows from the fact that

\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
and

(S_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{H}\cap S_{q})=\mathcal{P}\setminus(Z_{\ell}\cup Z^{\star}\cup Z_{q})
, where

Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
(because we are considering an
asynchronous
network) and
Z_{\ell},Z_{q}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
(follows from the construction of
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}
).
Let
P_{j}\in(S_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{H}\cap S_{q})
.
Hence at the time of the initialization,
(h,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, which further implies that

(h,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}\subseteq\mathsf{SIS}^{(S_{\ell})}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the time of initialization.
And hence
(h,q)
must have been removed from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
by the end of Phase I, since
P_{j}
would have been selected as the summand-sharing party in one of the hops during Phase I.
The same logic also applies to any arbitrary
(p,h)
, implying that
(p,h)
would have been removed from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
by the end of Phase I itself.

We next show that if all the parties behave honestly in the protocol, the parties output a linear-secret sharing of
a\cdot b
with IC-signatures.

Lemma
11
.

If no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
and if all parties in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
behave honestly, then in protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, the honest parties output
a linear secret-sharing of
a\cdot b
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, irrespective of the network type.

Proof

Note that
(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cap\mathcal{GD})=\emptyset
, which follows from Lemma
7
.
Now
Consider an
arbitrary

(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. We claim that
irrespective
of the network type, if all the parties behave honestly,
then the summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
is considered on behalf of
exactly
one party while
secret-sharing the summand-sums. That is there is
exactly
one
P_{j}
across the three phases,
such that the following hold:

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\ldots+[a]_{p}[b]_{q}+\ldots.

Assuming that the claim is true, the proof then follows from the linearity property of secret-sharing and the fact that
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=c^{(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+\ldots+c^{(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
holds. That is, corresponding to each
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the value

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
is secret-shared through an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, which guarantees that

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
is linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
Moreover, by the end of Phase II, the parties will be publicly knowing the set
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, which follows from the security properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.
Furthermore, after Phase II, the parties will be publicly reconstructing the shares
[a]_{p}
and
[b]_{q}
corresponding to every
(p,q)
, which are still present in
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.
Since corresponding to each

P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the parties take the default linear secret-sharing of

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
with IC-signatures and
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, it follows that

c
will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures and
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
Moreover,
c=a\cdot b
holds, since each summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
is considered exactly once, as per our claim.
We now proceed to prove our claim.

We first show that there exists
at least one

P_{j}
in one of the three phases, such that the following holds:

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\ldots+[a]_{p}[b]_{q}+\ldots.

For this, consider the following cases.

–

Case 1 — During Phase I, there is some
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, such that
(p,q)
was present in the set
\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
when
P_{j}

was added to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
: In this case, there is nothing to show.

–

Case 2 — At the end of Phase I,
(p,q)
is still
present
in
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
:
This implies that at the end of Phase I,
(\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\cap S_{p}\cap S_{q})=\emptyset
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
satisfy the condition
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
,
it follows that
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})\neq\emptyset
. Let
P_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\min(S_{p},S_{q})
.
Note that
P_{j}\not\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of Phase I, otherwise
(p,q)
would have been
removed
from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, which is a contradiction. Now if

P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{GD}
, then clearly the summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
will be present in
c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, shared by
P_{j}
during the Phase II.
Else, during Phase III, the parties will publicly reconstruct
[a]_{p}
and
[b]_{q}
and consequently
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
will be present in the default secret-sharing of
c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, taken on the
behalf of
P_{j}
.

To complete the proof of our claim, we next show that
(p,q)

cannot
be present in the summand-sum of
more than one
party across the three phases.
On
contrary
, let
P_{j}
and
P_{k}
be two
distinct
parties, such that the following holds across the three phases:

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\ldots+[a]_{p}[b]_{q}+\ldots\quad\wedge\quad c^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\ldots+[a]_{p}[b]_{q}+\ldots

Now there are three following cases.

–

Case 1 -
P_{j},P_{k}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of Phase I
: From the security properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
, the parties will
agree on which party to add to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during every hop during Phase I.
Moreover, from the protocol steps, exactly one party is selected as a summand-sharing party and added to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
in each hop.
Suppose that
P_{j}
was added to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during
\mathsf{hop}^{(j)}
, and that
P_{k}
was added to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during
\mathsf{hop}^{(k)}
.
Moreover, without loss of generality, let
\mathsf{hop}^{(j)}<\mathsf{hop}^{(k)}
. From the protocol steps, it follows that
P_{j},P_{k}\not\in\mathcal{GD}
, as otherwise no honest party would have voted for
P_{j}

and
P_{k}
as a candidate summand-sharing party and consequently,
P_{j},P_{k}\not\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.
Now as per the lemma conditions, all the parties (including
P_{k}
) behave honestly. Hence, the ordered pair

(p,q)
would be removed from
\mathsf{SIS}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of
\mathsf{hop}^{(j)}
.
Consequently,
P_{k}
will
not include
the summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
while computing
c^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during hop number
\mathsf{hop}^{(k)}
, which is a contradiction.

–

Case 2 —
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of Phase I and
P_{k}\notin\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of Phase I
:
In this case,
(p,q)
will be
removed
from
\mathsf{SIS}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of Phase I.
Hence, it
cannot
get re-assigned to any other party
P_{k}
after Phase I and hence
cannot
belong to
\mathsf{SIS}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.
Consequently, the summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
will
not
be considered while computing
c^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, which is a contradiction,

–

Case 3 -
P_{j}\notin\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
P_{k}\notin\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the end of Phase I
:
In this case, the summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{p}
is
deterministically
and
statically
re-assigned to the least-indexed party from the set
(S_{p}\cap S_{q})
, as per the

\min
function. Hence
(p,q)
will be re-assigned to either
P_{j}
or
P_{k}
, but
not both
.
Consequently, the summand
[a]_{p}\cdot[b]_{q}
will be considered while computing either
c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
or
c^{(k)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, but not both, which is a contradiction.

Lemma
12
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{5}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{6}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

Proof

During Phase I, there can be
\mathcal{O}(n)
hops, where during each hop, a party from
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
secret-shares a field element through an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
. Moreover,

n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
are invoked to agree upon the summand-sharing party of the hop.
Hence,
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
are required during Phase I.
During Phase II,
\mathcal{O}(n)
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
and
\mathcal{O}(n)
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
are required.
Finally during Phase III, up to
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|)
shares need to be publicly reconstructed.
The communication complexity now follows from the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
(Lemma
6
) and
communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}
with
|\mathcal{R}|=n
(Lemma
3
).

As a corollary of Lemma
12
, we derive the following corollary, which determines the
maximum
number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
which are invoked during an instance
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
. Looking ahead, this will be useful to later calculate the maximum number of instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
which need to be invoked as part of our final multiplication protocol.
This will be further useful to determine the number of linearly secret-shared values with IC-signatures and core-sets
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
, which need to be generated through the
protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
beforehand.

Corollary
1
.

During any instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, there can be at most
n^{2}+n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked.

Proof

The proof follows from the fact that during Phase I, there can be up to
n^{2}
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, if
\mathcal{GD}=\emptyset

and during Phase II, there can be up to
n-1
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, if only one party is added to
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during Phase I.

0.F.3
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
(see Fig
12
for the formal description).
We begin by showing that irrespective of the network type, the (honest) parties compute linearly secret-shared
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
with IC-signatures, which are random from the point of view of the adversary.

Lemma
13
.

In protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
, the following hold.

–

Synchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, honest parties will have linearly secret-shared
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, by the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
. Moreover, adversary’s view is independent of

a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

–

Asynchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely, honest parties will eventually have linearly secret-shared
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Moreover, adversary’s view is independent of

a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

Proof

We first consider a
synchronous
network. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and
let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}

be the set of
honest
parties.
Corresponding to each
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
,
the honest parties compute the output
[a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
during the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
at the time

T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. This follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
synchronous
network (Lemma
6
).
Consequently, at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will be present in the set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of every
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
.
Hence corresponding to each
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, each
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}

starts participating with input
1
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
at the time

T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
. Hence from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validity
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-guaranteed liveness
properties of

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), it follows that at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
compute the output
1
during the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
,
corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
. Consequently, at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will start participating in the remaining
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances for which no input has been provided yet (if there are any).
And from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-guaranteed liveness
and
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-consistency
properties
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
synchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
),
these
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances will produce common outputs for every honest party by the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
.
Hence, at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, the honest parties will have a common
\mathcal{CS}
, where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}

and where
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{CS}
.
We next wish to show that corresponding to
every

P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
,
there exists some quadruplet of values, which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

Consider an
arbitrary
party
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
. If
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
,
then whatever we wish to show is correct, as shown above.
Next, consider a
corrupt

P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
. Since
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
, it follows that the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
produces the output
1

for all honest parties.
This further implies that at least one

honest

P_{i}
must have computed some output during the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
, by the time

T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
(implying that
P_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
) and participated
with input
1
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
. Otherwise,

all honest
parties would participate with input

0
in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
and
then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-validit
y of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
synchronous
network, every honest party would have
computed the output
0
in the instance

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
and hence
P_{j}
will not be present in
\mathcal{CS}
, which is a contradiction.
Now if
P_{i}
has computed
some output during the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, then from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-commitment
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the

synchronous
network (Lemma
6
), it follows that except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
there exist values
(a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}})
, which will be
linearly secret-shared with IC-signature, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets,
by the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}+\Delta
.
Since
\Delta<T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, it follows that by the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
,
the honest parties will have
[a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
.
From the linearity property of secret-sharing, it then follows that by the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
,
the values
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

From the
privacy
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
(Lemma
6
), the view of the adversary will be independent of the values

(a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}})
, corresponding to the parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
. As
(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{CS})\neq\emptyset
,
it follows that
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be indeed random from the point of view of the adversary. This completes the proof for the case of
synchronous
network.

We next consider an
asynchronous
network.
Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and
let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}

be the set of
honest
parties. Notice that
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, since
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Now irrespective of the way messages are scheduled, there
will be eventually a subset of parties
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
participate with input
1
in the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
, corresponding to the parties in

\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
. This is because,
corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}

eventually
compute
some output during the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
which follows from the

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
6
).
So even if the
corrupt
parties
P_{j}
do not invoke their respective instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
,
there will be a set of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances corresponding to the parties in
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z
for some
Z\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
,
in which all
the parties in
\mathcal{H}
eventually participate with input
1
.
Consequently, from the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-almost-surely liveness
and
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-consistency
properties of

\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Theorem
3.1
), these
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances
eventually produce the output
1
for all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
.
Hence, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
eventually participate with some input in the remaining
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
instances,
which almost-surely produce some output for every honest party eventually.
From the properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
in the
asynchronous
network,
it then follows that all the honest
parties output the same
\mathcal{CS}
.

Now consider an
arbitrary

P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
. It implies that the honest parties computed the output
1
during
the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
, which further implies that at least one

honest

P_{i}
participated with input
1
in
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
after computing
its output in the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
. If
P_{j}
is
honest
, then the

\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
asynchronous
network
guarantees that except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the values

(a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}})

will be
eventually
linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
On the other hand, even if
P_{j}
is
corrupt
, the
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-commitment

of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
6
)
guarantees that there exist values
(a^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}},r^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}})
which are

eventually
linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
From the linearity property of secret-sharing, it then follows that eventually,
the values
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
The privacy of
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
is similar as for the synchronous communication network and the fact that

(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{CS})\neq\emptyset
still holds in the
asynchronous
network.

We next claim that all honest parties will eventually agree on whether the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
in
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
have succeeded or failed.

Lemma
14
.

Consider an arbitrary
\mathsf{iter}
,
such that all honest parties participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
,
where
no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
.
Then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, all honest parties
reconstruct a (common) value
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
and set
\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}
to a common Boolean value,
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+3T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network, or eventually in an asynchronous network.

Proof

From Lemma
13
, the honest parties have
[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[r_{\mathsf{iter}}]
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
in a synchronous network, or eventually in an asynchronous network,
except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
From Lemma
9
, it follows that the honest parties have

\{[c^{(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\ldots,[c^{(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]\}
and

\{[c^{\prime(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],\ldots,\allowbreak[c^{\prime(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]\}
from the
corresponding instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, either after time
T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
or eventually, based on the network type, where

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
are the underlying core-sets.
From Lemma
4
,
the honest parties reconstruct
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
from the corresponding instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
after time
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a
synchronous
network or eventually in an
asynchronous
network,
except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
From the linearity property of secret-sharing, it then follows that the honest parties
compute
[e_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and hence reconstruct
e_{\mathsf{iter}}

from the corresponding instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
, after time
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a
synchronous
network or eventually in an
asynchronous
network, except with probability

\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
. Moreover,

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
will be the underlying core-sets for
[e_{\mathsf{iter}}]
.
Again, from the linearity property of secret-sharing, it follows that
the honest parties compute
[d_{\mathsf{iter}}]
with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being
the underlying core-sets, followed by reconstructing
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
from the corresponding instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
,
which takes
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
time in a
synchronous
network or happens eventually in an
asynchronous
network.
Thus, the honest parties will have
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
either at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+3T_{\mathsf{Rec}}

in a synchronous network, or eventually in an asynchronous network.
Now depending upon the value of
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the honest parties set
\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}
to either
0
or
1
.

We next claim that if no cheating occurs, then the honest parties output a multiplication-triple, which is linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures.

Lemma
15
.

Consider an arbitrary
\mathsf{iter}
,
such that all honest parties participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
,
where
no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
.
If no party in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
behaves maliciously, then
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0

and
the honest parties output
([a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])

at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+3T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network or eventually in an asynchronous network, where
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}

and where
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
are
the underlying core-sets

Proof

If no party in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
behaves maliciously, then from Lemma
11
, the honest parties compute

[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
from the respective instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
, such that
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and

c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
holds
and where
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
are
the underlying core-sets.
Moreover, from Lemma
14
, the honest parties will compute

d_{\mathsf{iter}}
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+3T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network or eventually in an asynchronous network.
Furthermore, if
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and

c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
holds, the value
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be
0
and consequently, the honest parties will output

([a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
will be
the underlying core-sets. This is because all the secret-shared values in the protocol are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures,
with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being
the underlying core-sets.

We next show that if
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
, then the honest parties include at least one
new
maliciously-corrupt party in the set
\mathcal{GD}
.

Lemma
16
.

Consider an arbitrary
\mathsf{iter}
,
such that all honest parties participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
,
where
no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
.
If
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
, then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
the honest parties update
\mathcal{GD}
by adding a new maliciously-corrupt
party in
\mathcal{GD}
, either at the time
T_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}=T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+4T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network or eventually in an asynchronous network.

Proof

Let
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
and let
\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
be the set of summand-sharing parties across the two instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
executed in
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
.
That is:

\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c}\cup\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}.

Note that
there exists no
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
such that
P_{j}\in\mathcal{GD}
, which follows from Lemma
7
.
We claim that there exists at least one party
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, such that corresponding to

c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the following holds:

r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}+\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}.

Assuming the above holds, the proof now follows from the fact that once the parties reconstruct

d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
, they proceed to reconstruct the shares

\{[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q},[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q},[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}\}_{S_{q}\in\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}}
through appropriate instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}

and the values
c^{(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}},\ldots,c^{(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}},c^{\prime(1)}_{\mathsf{iter}},\ldots,c^{\prime(n)}_{\mathsf{iter}}
through
appropriate instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
. From Lemma
14
, Lemma
3
and Lemma
4
,
this happens by the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+4T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network or eventually in an asynchronous network,
except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
Upon reconstructing these values, party
P_{j}
will be included in the set

\mathcal{GD}
. Moreover, it is easy to see that
P_{j}
is a maliciously-corrupt party since, for every
honest

P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}.

We prove the above claim through a contradiction. Let the following condition hold for
each

P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
:

r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}+\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}.

Next, summing the above equation over all
P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, we get that the following holds:

\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}}r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}}r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}+\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}.

This implies that the following holds:

r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}}c^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime(j)}_{\mathsf{iter}}=r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot\sum_{P_{j}\in\mathsf{Selected}_{\mathsf{iter}}}\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}+\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{SIS}^{(j)}_{\mathsf{iter},c^{\prime}}}[a_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{p}[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]_{q}.

Now based on the way
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
are defined, the above implies that the following holds:

r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=r\cdot a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}+a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}

This further implies that

r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=(r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}+b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}})\cdot a_{\mathsf{iter}}

Since in the protocol
e_{\mathsf{iter}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}+b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, the above implies that

r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c_{\mathsf{iter}}+c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=e_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot a_{\mathsf{iter}}\quad\Rightarrow\;e_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot a_{\mathsf{iter}}-r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c_{\mathsf{iter}}-c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=0\quad\Rightarrow\;d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0,

where the last equality follows from the fact that in the protocol,
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}e_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot a_{\mathsf{iter}}-r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c_{\mathsf{iter}}-c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
.
However
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
is a contradiction since, according to the hypothesis of the lemma, we are given that

d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
.

We next show that if the honest parties output a secret-shared triple in the protocol, then except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
, the triple is a multiplication-triple.
Moreover, the triple will be random for the adversary.

Lemma
17
.

Consider an arbitrary
\mathsf{iter}
,
such that all honest parties participate in the instance
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a},\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}},{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},\mathcal{GD},\mathsf{iter})
,
where
no honest party is present in
\mathcal{GD}
.
If
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
, then the honest parties output linearly secret-shared

([a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])
with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being
the underlying core-sets,
at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+3T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network or eventually in an asynchronous network where,
except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
, the condition
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
holds.
Moreover, the view of
\mathsf{Adv}
will be independent of
(a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}})
.

Proof

Let
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
. From Lemma
9
, all honest parties will agree that
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
, either at the time
T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+3T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
in a synchronous network or eventually in an asynchronous network. Then, from the protocol steps, the honest parties output
([a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])
, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being
the underlying core-sets.
In the protocol
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}e_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot a_{\mathsf{iter}}-r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot c_{\mathsf{iter}}-c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
, where

e_{\mathsf{iter}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}r_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}+b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
. Since
d_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
holds, it implies that the honest parties have verified that the following holds:

r_{\mathsf{iter}}(a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}-c_{\mathsf{iter}})=(c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}-a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}).

We note that
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be a random element from
\mathbb{F}
and will be unknown to
\mathsf{Adv}
till it is publicly reconstructed, which follows from Lemma
13

We also note that
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be unknown to
\mathsf{Adv}
, till the outputs for the underlying instances
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
are computed, and the honest parties have
[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
. This is because, in the protocol,
the honest parties start participating in the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
to reconstruct
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
, only after they compute

[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
.
Now we have the following cases with respect to
whether any party from
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{GD}
behaved maliciously during the underlying instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
.

–

Case I:
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
— In this case,
(a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}})
is a multiplication-triple.

–

Case II:
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
, but
c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
— This case is never possible, as this will lead to the contradiction
that
r_{\mathsf{iter}}(a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}-c_{\mathsf{iter}})\neq(c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}-a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}})
holds.

–

Case III:
c_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
, but
c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
— This case is possible only if
r_{\mathsf{iter}}=0
, as otherwise
this will lead to the contradiction
that
r_{\mathsf{iter}}(a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}-c_{\mathsf{iter}})\neq(c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}-a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}})
holds. However, since
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
is a random element from
\mathbb{F}
, it implies that this case
can occur only with probability at most
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.

–

Case IV:
c_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}
as well as
c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
— This case is possible only if

r_{\mathsf{iter}}=(c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}-a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}})\cdot(a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}-c_{\mathsf{iter}})^{-1}
, as otherwise
this will lead to the contradiction
that
r_{\mathsf{iter}}(a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b_{\mathsf{iter}}-c_{\mathsf{iter}})\neq(c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}-a_{\mathsf{iter}}\cdot b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}})
holds. However, since
r_{\mathsf{iter}}
is a random element from
\mathbb{F}
, it implies that this case
can occur only with probability at most
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.

Hence, we have shown that except with probability at most
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
, the triple
(a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}})
is a multiplication-triple.
To complete the proof, we need to argue that the view of
\mathsf{Adv}
in the protocol will be independent of the triple

(a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}})
. For this, we first note that
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be random for the adversary
at the time of their generation, which follows from Lemma
13
.
From Lemma
10
,
\mathsf{Adv}
learns nothing additional
about
a_{\mathsf{iter}}
,
b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
during the two instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
.
While
\mathsf{Adv}
learns the value of
e_{\mathsf{iter}}
, since
b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
is a uniformly distributed for
\mathsf{Adv}
,
for every candidate value of
b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}
from the view-point of
\mathsf{Adv}
,
there is a corresponding value of
b_{\mathsf{iter}}
consistent with the
e_{\mathsf{iter}}
learnt by
\mathsf{Adv}
.
Hence, learning
e_{\mathsf{iter}}
does not add any new information about
(a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}})
to the view of
\mathsf{Adv}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{Adv}
will be knowing beforehand that
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
will be
0
and hence, learning this value does not change
the view of
\mathsf{Adv}
regarding
(a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}},c_{\mathsf{iter}})
.

We next derive the communication complexity of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
.

Lemma
18
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{5}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{6}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

Proof

To generate
[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[r_{\mathsf{iter}}]
,
\mathcal{O}(n)
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
are invoked. To compute
[c_{\mathsf{iter}}]
and
[c^{\prime}_{\mathsf{iter}}]
, two instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}

are invoked. To publicly reconstruct
e_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
d_{\mathsf{iter}}
, two instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
are invoked with
|\mathcal{R}|=n
. Finally, if
d_{\mathsf{iter}}\neq 0
, then
3\cdot|\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s}}|
instances of

\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}
and
2n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
are invoked, with
|\mathcal{R}|=n
. The communication complexity now follows from the communication complexity of

\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
(Lemma
6
), communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}
(Lemma
12
),
communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RecShare}}
(Lemma
3
) and communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
(Lemma
4
).

0.F.4
Properties of the Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
(see Fig
13
for the formal details.)
We begin by showing that each party computes an output in the protocol.

Lemma
19
.

Let
t
be the size of the largest set in
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
. Then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, the honest parties compute an output during

\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, by the time
T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}=(t+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
in a synchronous network, or almost-surely, eventually in an asynchronous network, where

T_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}=T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+T_{\mathsf{BasicMult}}+4T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
.

Proof

From Lemma
14
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
the honest parties will know the outcome of each iteration
\mathsf{iter}
, since all honest parties set the Boolean variable

\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}
to a common value. For every iteration

\mathsf{iter}
where
\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}
is set to
1
, from Lemma
16
, a
new
corrupt party is added to
\mathcal{GD}
. Thus, after at most
t
iterations,
all the corrupt parties will be included in
\mathcal{GD}
and the parties will set

\mathsf{flag}_{\mathsf{iter}}
to
0
in the next iteration. Moreover, they will output

{({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])}
, computed during the corresponding instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
.

We next claim that the output computed by the honest parties is indeed a multiplication-triple.

Lemma
20
.

If the honest parties output
{({\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|},[a_{\mathsf{iter}}],[b_{\mathsf{iter}}],[c_{\mathsf{iter}}])}
during the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
,
then
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c_{\mathsf{iter}}
are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Moreover,
c_{\mathsf{iter}}=a_{\mathsf{iter}}b_{\mathsf{iter}}
holds, except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.
Furthermore, the view of the adversary remains independent of
a_{\mathsf{iter}},b_{\mathsf{iter}}
and
c_{\mathsf{iter}}
.

Proof

Follows from Lemma
17
.

We finally derive the communication complexity of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
.

Lemma
21
.

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
incurs a communication of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|\cdot n^{6}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}+n^{7}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

Proof

The proof follows from the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
(Lemma
18
) and the fact that
\mathcal{O}(n)
instances of

\Pi_{\mathsf{RandMultCI}}
are invoked in the protocol.

Appendix 0.G
Properties of the Circuit Evaluation Protocol

In this section, we prove the properties of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
(see Fig
14
for the formal description).
We begin by showing that the honest parties compute some output during the pre-processing phase.

Lemma 58

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
achieves the following during the pre-processing phase.

–

Synchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}
, the honest parties have

\{r^{(\mathfrak{l})}\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,L}
, which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, where
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
. Moreover, the view of the adversary remains independent of
the values
\{r^{(\mathfrak{l})}\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,L}
.
At the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, the honest parties have triples
\{(a^{(\ell)},b^{(\ell)},c^{(\ell)})\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,c_{M}}
,
which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, where
c^{(\ell)}=a^{(\ell)}\cdot b^{(\ell)}
, except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.
The view of the adversary will be independent of the multiplication-triples.

–

Asynchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely, the honest parties eventually have

\{r^{(\mathfrak{l})}\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,L}
, which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, where
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
. Moreover, the view of the adversary remains independent of the
values
\{r^{(\mathfrak{l})}\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,L}
. Furthermore, the honest parties eventually have
triples
\{(a^{(\ell)},b^{(\ell)},c^{(\ell)})\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,c_{M}}
,
which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, where
c^{(\ell)}=a^{(\ell)}\cdot b^{(\ell)}
, except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.
The view of the adversary will be independent of the multiplication-triples.

Proof

The proof follows from the
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
-correctness
,
\mathcal{Z}_{a}
-correctness
and
privacy
of the protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
(Theorem
7.2
) and
from the properties of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
in the asynchronous and asynchronous network (Lemma
19
and Lemma
20
).
We also note that the multiplication-triples will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. This is because there will be at most
n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}

invoked as part of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
for generating
c_{M}
multiplication-triples. And prior to invoking the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, the honest parties would have already generated

n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
number of linearly secret-shared random pads with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying
core-sets through the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
, which
can serve
n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
.

We next show that during the input phase, the inputs of all honest parties will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures in a
synchronous
network
and in an
asynchronous
network, the inputs of a subset of the parties will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures.

Lemma 59

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
achieves the following during the input phase.

–

Synchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
,
the honest parties will have a common subset
\mathcal{CS}
where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that all honest parties will be present in
\mathcal{CS}
.
Moreover, corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
, there will be some value, say
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}
, which is the same as
x^{(j)}
, which will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures,
with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Furthermore, the view of the adversary will be independent of the
x^{(j)}
values, corresponding to the
honest parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
.

–

Asynchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely,
the honest parties will eventually have a common subset
\mathcal{CS}
where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
Moreover, corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
, there will be some value, say
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}
, which is the same as
x^{(j)}
, which will be eventually linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures,
with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. Furthermore, the view of the adversary will be independent of the
x^{(j)}
values, corresponding to the
honest parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
.

Proof

We first consider a
synchronous
network.
From the protocol steps, the honest parties start participating in the input phase, only after computing output during the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, which
from Lemma
58
happens at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
. We also note that there can be at most

n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, invoked as part of the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
, which will utilize the
secret-shared values
\{r^{(\mathfrak{l})}\}_{\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}}
as pads.
Consequently, the remaining linearly secret-shared values
\{r^{(\mathfrak{l})}\}_{\mathfrak{l}=n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+1,\ldots,n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n}

will be still available to the honest parties for being used as pads in up to
n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, since these pads will be random from the adversary’s point of view.

Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and
let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}

be the set of
honest
parties.
We claim that by the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have a common subset
\mathcal{CS}
, where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}

and where
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{CS}
. The proof for this is exactly the same as that of Lemma
13
. Namely, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}
, all
the parties in
\mathcal{H}
would start participating with input
1
in the BA instances
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}^{(j)}
, corresponding to the parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{H}
, since by this time, the instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by the
parties in
\mathcal{H}
will produce output for all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
. Consequently, these BA instances
will produce output
1
at the time

T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, after which all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will start participating with input
0
in the remaining BA instances (if any).
Consequently, by the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
, all the
n
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
will produce some output and the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have a common
\mathcal{CS}
.
Next, it can be shown that corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
, there exists some value, say
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}
, which is the same as
x^{(j)}
,
such that the parties in
\mathcal{H}
have a linear secret-sharing with IC-signatures of
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}
,
with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. The proof for this follows similar lines as that of Lemma
13
.
It is easy to see that
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}
will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
This is because the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
invoked by
P_{j}
utilizes
r^{(n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+j)}
as the pad, which is linearly secret-shared
with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.

The proof of the lemma for an
asynchronous
network is almost the same as above and follows using similar arguments as used to prove Lemma
13

for the case of
asynchronous
network.

Finally, the privacy of the inputs
x^{(j)}
of the parties
P_{j}\in(\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{CS})

follows from the
privacy
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
and the fact that the underlying pads
r^{(n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+j)}
used in the corresponding
instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
are still random for the adversary, after the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
.

We next show that the honest parties compute an output during the circuit-evaluation phase.

Lemma 60

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
achieves the following during the circuit-evaluation phase, where
D_{M}
denotes the multiplicative depth of
\mathsf{ckt}
.

–

Synchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+(D_{M}+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
,
the honest parties will have
y
, where
y=f({x^{\star}}^{(1)},\ldots,{x^{\star}}^{(n)})
, such that
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}=x^{(j)}
for every honest party
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
and where

{x^{\star}}^{(j)}=0
for every
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{CS}
