. Moreover, all honest parties will be present in
\mathcal{CS}
.
Furthermore, the view of the adversary will be independent of the
x^{(j)}
values, corresponding to the
honest parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
.

–

Asynchronous Network
: Except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely,
the honest parties will eventually have
y
, where
y=f({x^{\star}}^{(1)},\ldots,{x^{\star}}^{(n)})
, where
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}=x^{(j)}
for every honest party
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
and where

{x^{\star}}^{(j)}=0
for every
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{CS}
. Furthermore, the view of the adversary will be independent of the
x^{(j)}
values, corresponding to the
honest parties
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
.

Proof

Let us first consider a
synchronous
network. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and
let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}

be the set of
honest
parties.
From Lemma
58
, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
,
the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have the triples
\{(a^{(\ell)},b^{(\ell)},c^{(\ell)})\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,c_{M}}
,
which are linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures, with

{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets and where
c^{(\ell)}=a^{(\ell)}\cdot b^{(\ell)}
, except with probability
\frac{1}{|\mathbb{F}|}
.
Moreover, from Lemma
59
, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}
,
the honest parties will have a common subset
\mathcal{CS}
where
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{CS}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, such that all honest parties will be present in
\mathcal{CS}
.
Furthermore, corresponding to every
P_{j}\in\mathcal{CS}
, there will be some value, say
{x^{\star}}^{(j)}
, which is the same as
x^{(j)}
for an

honest

P_{j}
, which will be linearly secret-shared with IC-signatures,
with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets. At the end of the input phase, the parties take
0
as the input on behalf of the parties
P_{j}\not\in\mathcal{CS}

and take the default linear secret-sharing of
0
with IC-signatures, with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
To prove the lemma, we show that all the gates in
\mathsf{ckt}
are correctly evaluated. Namely, for every gate in
\mathsf{ckt}
, given the gate-inputs in a linearly secret-shared fashion
with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets, the parties compute the gate-output in a linearly secret-shared fashion
with IC-signatures with
{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{1},\ldots,{\bf{\mathcal{GW}}}_{|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|}
being the underlying core-sets.
While this is true for the linear gates in
\mathsf{ckt}
, which follows from the linearity of the secret-sharing,
the same is true even for the multiplication gates, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
This is because, for every multiplication gate, the parties deploy a linearly secret-shared multiplication-triple from the pre-processing phase and apply Beaver’s method.
And the masked gate-inputs are correctly reconstructed through instances of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
Since all the independent multiplication gates at the same multiplicative depth can be evaluated in parallel, to evaluate the multiplication gates, it will take a total
D_{M}\cdot T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
time.
Finally, once the circuit-output is ready in a secret-shared fashion, it is publicly reconstructed through an instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rec}}
, which takes
T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
time
and produces the correct output, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
.
The
privacy
of the inputs of the honest parties in
\mathcal{CS}
follows from the
privacy
of
\Pi_{\mathsf{LSh}}
(Lemma
6
)
and the fact no additional information is revealed during the evaluation of multiplication gates. This is because the underlying multiplication-triples which are deployed while
applying Beaver’s method are random for the adversary.

The proof for the case of
asynchronous
network follows similar arguments as above and depends upon the properties of the pre-processing phase and input phase
in the
asynchronous
network (Lemma
58
and Lemma
59
).

We finally show that the honest parties terminate the protocol.

Lemma 61

If the network is synchronous, then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
,
the honest parties terminate the protocol at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+(D_{M}+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{Rec}}+\Delta
.
If the network is asynchronous, then except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely,
the honest parties eventually terminate the protocol.

Proof

Let us first consider a
synchronous
network. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and
let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}

be the set of
honest
parties.
From Lemma
60
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, at the time
T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+(D_{M}+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{Rec}}
,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have
y
. Hence
every party in
\mathcal{H}
will send a
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y
to all the parties, which gets delivered within
\Delta
time, while
the parties in
Z^{\star}
may send a
\mathsf{ready}
message for some
y^{\prime}
where
y^{\prime}\neq y
. Now since

\mathcal{Z}_{s}

does not
satisfy the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(Z^{\star},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition, it follows that no party in
\mathcal{H}
will ever send a
\mathsf{ready}
message for any
y^{\prime}
where
y^{\prime}\neq y
.
As
\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, it follows that at the time

T_{\mathsf{Rand}}+T_{\mathsf{TripGen}}+T_{\mathsf{LSh}}+2T_{\mathsf{BA}}+(D_{M}+1)\cdot T_{\mathsf{Rec}}+\Delta
, all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will have sufficient number of
\mathsf{ready}
messages for
y
and hence they terminate
with output
y
.

Next, consider an
asynchronous
network. Let
Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{a}
be the set of
corrupt
parties and
let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}

be the set of
honest
parties. Note that
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, since
\mathcal{Z}_{a}\subset\mathcal{Z}_{s}
.
From Lemma
60
, except with probability
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\cdot\mathsf{\epsilon_{ICP}})
, almost-surely,
all the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually compute
y
. Hence
every party in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually send some
\mathsf{ready}
message. We claim that no party in
\mathcal{H}
will ever send a
\mathsf{ready}
message for any
y^{\prime}\neq y
.
On the contrary, let
P_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
be the
first
party, which sends a
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y^{\prime}\neq y
.
From the protocol steps, it follows that
P_{i}
sends the
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y^{\prime}
after computing
y^{\prime}
during the circuit-evaluation phase.
Otherwise, there should exist a subset of parties
{\mathcal{}A}
where
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
satisfies
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}({\mathcal{}A},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition (implying that
{\mathcal{}A}
has at least one party from
\mathcal{H}
),
who should have sent the
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y^{\prime}
to
P_{i}
, which is
not
possible, since we are assuming
P_{i}
to be
first
party from
\mathcal{H}
to send a
\mathsf{ready}

message for
y^{\prime}
. From Lemma
60
,
P_{i}
will
not
compute
y^{\prime}
and hence will
not
send a
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y^{\prime}
.
Now since every party in
\mathcal{H}
eventually computes
y
in the circuit-evaluation phase, it eventually sends a
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y
. And since

\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
and
\mathcal{P}\setminus Z^{\star}\not\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, it follows that irrespective of the behaviour of the corrupt parties, the parties in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually
receive a sufficient number of
\mathsf{ready}
messages for
y
, to terminate with output
y
.

Let
P_{h}
be the
first
party from
\mathcal{H}
, who terminates with output
y
. This implies that
there exists a subset of parties
{\mathcal{}W}
with
\mathcal{P}\setminus{\mathcal{}W}\in\mathcal{Z}_{s}
, who sends a
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y
to
P_{h}
. Now consider the set
(\mathcal{H}\cap{\mathcal{}W})
.
The set satisfies the
\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}(\mathcal{H}\cap{\mathcal{}W},\mathcal{Z}_{s})
condition, due to the
\mathbb{Q}^{(2,1)}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Z}_{s},\mathcal{Z}_{a})
condition.
The
\mathsf{ready}
messages of these parties (for
y
) get eventually delivered to every party in
\mathcal{H}
.
Consequently, every party in
\mathcal{H}
(including
P_{h}
) who has
not
yet sent any
\mathsf{ready}
message will eventually send the
\mathsf{ready}
message for
y
, which gets eventually delivered to all the parties.
And as a result, every party in
\mathcal{H}
will eventually have a sufficient number of
\mathsf{ready}
messages for
y
, to terminate with the output
y
.

We next derive the communication complexity of the protocol.

Lemma 62

Protocol
\Pi_{\mathsf{cktEval}}
incurs a communication of

\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{Z}_{s}|^{2}\cdot n^{12}\cdot\log{|\mathbb{F}|}\cdot|\sigma|)

bits and makes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
calls to
\Pi_{\mathsf{BA}}
.

Proof

The communication complexity is dominated by the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
to generate
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle def}}{{=}}n^{3}\cdot c_{M}+4n^{2}\cdot c_{M}+n^{2}+n
random secret-shared values
and the instance of
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
to generate
L=c_{M}
number of secret-shared multiplication-triples. The proof now follows from the communication complexity of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Rand}}
(Theorem
7.2
)
and the communication complexity of the (generalized)
\Pi_{\mathsf{TripGen}}
protocol (Lemma
21
).

Theorem
9.1
now easily follows from Lemma
58
-
62
.
